18 Multi-Agent Coordination

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why can splitting a task across several agents cost more and deliver less than giving one agent the same resources?

Splitting a task across several agents looks like the natural answer when the work is wide, since each worker can hold a smaller context and pursue its piece in parallel. The split replaces nothing the single agent already had. A capable single controller already runs searches and tests concurrently, keeps its context lean through retrieval and compaction, and holds scoped grants, so delegation must be judged against that baseline rather than against a fragile loop. What delegation adds is a set of boundaries between separately managed trajectories, and each boundary must carry explicitly what one trajectory carried implicitly. A worker can build against an interface that changes before its edit lands, two patches can merge cleanly and still break each other, reviewers that share a model can agree on the same mistake, and a canceled child can leave a container running and a budget half spent. Coordination overhead grows with every worker added, while the serial part of the task caps any speedup the parallel part can buy. Whether to split is therefore a measured decision. A multi-agent design earns its place only when it delivers more accepted tasks per unit of cost, or finishes them sooner in a way the objective values, than a single controller given the same tools and budget. Every handoff multiplies horizon, state, and authority, and the runtime must keep each delegated obligation owned until it settles.

Learning Objectives
  • Estimate the speedup and useful worker count of a work partition from its serial fraction and coordination overhead
  • Calculate makespan bounds from a task’s dependency graph to decide whether added slots or shorter tasks pay
  • Design bounded receipts and credit windows that cap returned context and payload occupancy, and size mean residence under an explicit freshness policy
  • Apply shared-write collision estimates to justify private workspaces and dependency revalidation before publication
  • Calculate majority false approval under correlated reviewer errors to choose between more reviewers and a different check
  • Evaluate fan-out completion rules and hedging under deadline, capacity, and duplicate-effect constraints
  • Apply spawn limits, restart policies, attenuated grants, and atomic reservations to bound a delegation tree’s fan-out, authority, and spending
  • Evaluate delegation against a capable single-controller baseline using a declared objective and matched resource ceilings
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

18.1 Splitting the Repair Agent’s Work

The repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) has so far fixed a connection-pool race, a change confined to one module that a single controller holds comfortably in one context. Its next assignment is wider: migrate an authentication module across a repository, a change that reaches every caller of the module’s interface and is exactly the kind of repair task that tempts a split. The task naturally splits into two coupled changes: revising an internal interface and updating its downstream callers. A single coordinator could retain the entire task, dispatching parallel searches and test runs to collect evidence before synthesizing a unified patch. Alternatively, it could partition the problem, assigning the interface update and caller modifications to separate workers. Each worker operates within a focused context window, editing candidate files and reacting to compiler diagnostics independently. This division introduces an immediate distributed consistency challenge: the caller modifications must remain compatible with the interface that ultimately lands, not merely the snapshot the caller worker read at dispatch.

A fleet multiplies horizon, state, and authority, so every handoff must preserve closure.

The runtime mechanisms established in earlier chapters govern a single execution trajectory at a time. Within that boundary, the harness checks each model and tool invocation against an assigned grant and token budget. Durable execution logs one trajectory’s event history, and failure recovery settles, repairs, or compensates the effects of that one trajectory. Evaluation compares trials of one configuration against another, and curation and post-training improve the policy that drives it. Partitioning the migration preserves these local controls inside each individual worker, but it leaves the coordination space between them unmanaged. A single-agent runtime provides no mechanism to resolve which interface revision the caller worker targets, whether a clean textual merge of two patches preserves semantic correctness, or what consensus among five reviewer agents actually guarantees. Nor does it define who claims an orphaned test container after a coordinator cancels a subtask, or how a parent bounds the authority and compute budget delegated to a child. Without explicit systems controls, every such gap threatens to make the split cost far more than it buys.

Making delegation governable requires runtime controls, and deciding whether it pays requires an empirical framework. The required controls are state records and verification gates owned directly by the runtime rather than left to prompt instructions. They include enforceable task assignments and receipts that separate an agent’s self-reported claims from verified tool evidence. They also include a publication barrier that revalidates cross-module dependencies before any candidate patch merges, clean cancellation paths that account for in-flight work, and authority grants that narrow monotonically at every delegation hop. These mechanisms pair with a measurement discipline that compares each multi-agent design against a single agent holding equivalent parallel tools and resource budgets, and that prices every token consumed by failed, retried, or canceled subtasks.

The migration carries the argument from the decision to split through the final accounting, and each control answers a hazard that the previous one exposes. A screen of serial work against coordination overhead first decides whether a partition can pay at all (section 18.2). Dependency, communication, and supervision structures then fix what may run concurrently and who owns each child (section 18.3), and typed envelopes and receipts fix what crosses each handoff (section 18.4). Once workers run in parallel, guarded publication keeps their edits consistent (section 18.5), and a joint error model shows what agreement among reviewers can and cannot certify (section 18.6). Cancellation, hedging, and credit windows bound the work still in flight (section 18.7), and attenuated grants and atomic reservations bound authority and spending at every hop (section 18.8). A matched comparison against a capable single controller decides whether the whole design earns its tax (section 18.9).

18.2 The Delegation Trade-Off

Handing the interface update and the caller update to dedicated workers differs from issuing concurrent model queries, and that difference defines the unit of delegation. A delegated worker owns a bounded child trajectory with its own context window, model-and-tool loop, task contract, and independent lifecycle. Several candidate samples dispatched by one controller, such as the self-consistency votes of test-time compute, are instead parallel calls inside one trajectory. They are the cheapest ensemble an agent can run and also the most correlated, because every sample shares the model, the prompt, and the input’s difficulty, a dependence that section 18.6 quantifies. The architectural shift lies in establishing explicit boundaries between separately managed trajectories, regardless of whether those workers execute on a single host or across a distributed cluster.

↩︎ Self-consistency, which samples several reasoning paths and returns the plurality answer, is established in Choosing a selection rule.

The point of comparison throughout this chapter is the capable baseline: the single-agent runtime developed in earlier chapters, which already manages its context window, reserves system resources, verifies proposed tool effects externally, and executes candidate searches and isolated tools concurrently. Delegation retains every one of those operational requirements while introducing new coordination boundaries. The systems question is whether dividing control and context improves a declared task objective enough to justify the overhead of managing them.

18.2.1 Motivations for delegation

When a single agent runs a wide repository migration inside one shared context and one broad authority grant, compiler diagnostics from one module crowd out the prompt needed for the next while every tool call carries permissions for the whole codebase. Context partitioning addresses the prompt growth directly: each worker retains only the material strictly necessary for its subtask. In the migration scenario, the interface worker maintains the target API contract and local unit tests without ingesting the intermediate compiler diagnostics of the caller worker, allowing each subsequent model invocation to process substantially fewer prompt tokens. However, the net token savings depend entirely on the structure of the decomposition. If both workers repeatedly require the full repository commit history, separate contexts duplicate prompt state rather than isolating it, a redundancy the single-agent baseline already avoids through retrieval and compaction.

Delegation also provides separate task ownership. Workers can pursue multi-turn exploratory investigations through iterative tool feedback and local reasoning loops while a central coordinator tracks inter-task dependencies and orchestrates final integration. Assigning task ownership, however, does not eliminate underlying data dependencies. A caller worker that generates code against an outdated interface must still reconcile its implementation once the interface worker finalizes the new contract. True operational independence derives from the causal graph of the task itself, never from cosmetic role descriptions in prompt templates.

Finally, decomposing work across workers enforces task-scoped authority, binding tools, execution capabilities, and resource budgets directly to individual subtasks. For example, a test-runner worker can be granted execution privileges in an isolated container without granting it credentials to publish pull requests or write to production databases. Even so, process isolation does not eliminate trust boundaries. A child worker’s textual output can still inject unvalidated claims or prompt injections into the parent coordinator’s context, and any external mutations it produces must still be verified and settled by the runtime. Table 1 separates these opportunities from capabilities already present in the baseline.

18.2.2 The coordination tax

Splitting the authentication migration across separate interface and caller workers adds work that a single controller never performs: the coordinator spends turns packaging task assignments and reconciling returned diffs, both workers ingest shared repository context, and final integration waits at the join barrier for the slower child to finish. Every delegation boundary incurs this coordination tax (principle 14).

Definition 18.1: Coordination tax

Coordination tax is the work and elapsed time a delegated design spends on handoffs rather than on the task: preparing and validating envelopes, delivering context, scheduling children, collecting receipts, and reconciling and integrating their results.

  1. Significance: The tax enters both terms of the cost per accepted task, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) (equation 1.2): its tokens and tool time raise \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]\), and the part on the critical path lengthens every attempt against its deadline.
  2. Distinction: While task decomposition aims to reduce makespan by distributing work concurrently, the coordination tax represents the unavoidable friction introduced by that distribution. This tax scales with worker count, communication frequency, and inter-task dependencies.
  3. Common pitfall: Accounting only for the tokens and execution time of successful worker turns. Context re-ingestion, supervisor arbitration turns, failed retries, and abandoned speculative branches all consume model quota and execution budgets.

Coordination tax impacts systems along two distinct dimensions: aggregate resource consumption and end-to-end latency. Duplicated input prompt tokens, redundant tool invocations, and serialize-deserialize cycles increase the total operational cost. Meanwhile, user-perceived delay is governed by makespan, the wall-clock time elapsed from the arrival of a task to the completion and verification of its final artifact. Makespan depends strictly on the operations situated on the critical path. A straggling child worker delays synchronization at the join barrier, while subsequent arbitration and reconciliation turns further inflate the critical path before results can be accepted.

Amdahl’s fixed-work argument1 gives the first screening model (Amdahl 1967). Let \(\SerialReferenceTime\) be the time for a fixed set of operations executed serially, with fraction \(\ParallelFraction\) perfectly divisible among \(\WorkerCount\) equally fast workers and fraction \(1-\ParallelFraction\) remaining serial. Assume enough resources for that division and let \(\CoordinationOverhead(\WorkerCount)\) be the additional elapsed overhead, normalized by \(\SerialReferenceTime\). Then

1 Amdahl’s Argument: Amdahl’s 1967 conference paper contains no equation; it argues in prose that the serial housekeeping of a program limits what any number of parallel processors can deliver, and the familiar formula was written down by later authors. The lesson transfers intact to delegation, where planning, integration, and final assessment are the housekeeping that no added worker absorbs.

\[\text{Speedup}(\WorkerCount)=\frac{\SerialReferenceTime}{\Time(\WorkerCount)}=\frac{1}{(1-\ParallelFraction)+\ParallelFraction/\WorkerCount+\CoordinationOverhead(\WorkerCount)}. \tag{1}\]

With no coordination overhead, \(\CoordinationOverhead(\WorkerCount)=0\), equation 1 reduces to Amdahl’s law, and the serial term alone caps speedup at \(1/(1-\ParallelFraction)\) for any number of workers. The equation provides a preliminary screening model for a specified work partition. The baseline \(\SerialReferenceTime\) reflects that partition executed sequentially on a single thread of execution, rather than the capable baseline runtime (which already pipelines tool executions and concurrent candidate samplings). In production, an empirical benchmark of the capable baseline must replace \(\SerialReferenceTime\) before finalizing any worker allocation. Within this theoretical formulation, achieving any net speedup over the serial reference requires that:

\[\CoordinationOverhead(\WorkerCount)<\ParallelFraction\left(1-\frac{1}{\WorkerCount}\right).\]

The right-hand side represents the theoretical wall-clock time saved by distributing the parallelizable portion across \(\WorkerCount\) workers. Whenever coordination overhead \(\CoordinationOverhead(\WorkerCount)\) exceeds this threshold, the split task takes longer than serial execution. Furthermore, this inequality constrains execution time alone. Task acceptance rates, token expenditures, and monetary costs enter the trade-off in section 18.9, which gives serializing work and pairwise reconciliation separate coefficients because their remedies differ (section 18.9.3).

Napkin Math 18.1: Screening a proposed work partition
Problem: A hypothetical fixed workload has \(\ParallelFraction=\) 0.80 and assumed normalized overhead \(\CoordinationOverhead(\WorkerCount)=\WorkerOverheadSlope(\WorkerCount-1)\) with \(\WorkerOverheadSlope=\) 0.02. The resource ceiling permits at most 16 workers. Which integer count minimizes modeled elapsed time?

Math: Evaluating the denominator of equation 1 for each permitted count, four workers require 0.460 of the serial reference time. The minimum occurs at 6 workers, requiring 0.433, a 2.3× speedup. Eight workers increase the ratio to 0.440, because the shrinking parallel term \(\ParallelFraction/\WorkerCount\) no longer pays for the added overhead. Halving the overhead coefficient to 0.01 moves the optimum to 9 workers and the speedup to 2.7×.

Systems insight: The serial fraction sets the ceiling and the overhead sets the useful fleet size. No worker count beats \(1/(1-\ParallelFraction)=\) 5× here, and the best count sits near \(\sqrt{\ParallelFraction/\WorkerOverheadSlope}\), so halving the per-worker overhead \(\WorkerOverheadSlope\) adds 3 workers but only 0.4 of speedup. Shrinking the serial planning and integration work is the larger lever.

A reduction in modeled makespan does not automatically justify a multi-agent architecture. Production systems optimize under varied constraints: one automated migration service may seek to maximize accepted pull requests prior to a nightly release window subject to a strict token budget, whereas another prioritizes minimizing the total cost per accepted migration. Trading latency for computational overhead is justified only against an explicit, pre-declared optimization objective, and the decision framework of section 18.9 charges that objective for retries and failed executions as well. Before sizing a worker pool, an engineer must map the causal dependency graph of the workload to see which subtasks can run concurrently and what evidence each downstream join requires. Those dependencies govern the coordination topology.

18.3 Coordination Topologies

In the authentication migration (migration_task_01), the interface worker and the caller worker can inspect the baseline snapshot (repo_base_01) in parallel, yet the caller’s patch still depends on the interface definitions that its peer is actively modifying. Routing both workers through a single supervisor does not eliminate that underlying causal dependency. A runtime architecture that naively treats the supervisor’s message-routing graph as the true dependency graph will prematurely accept and publish a caller patch built on stale interface assumptions.

To build reliable multi-agent systems, engineers must distinguish three separate structural graphs that govern execution:

  1. The dependency graph defines causal precedence by recording which artifacts, decisions, or validation checks must complete before downstream work can safely proceed.
  2. The communication topology defines the network paths, protocols, and message envelopes through which agents exchange information.
  3. The supervision tree defines operational ownership over agent lifecycles, governing fault isolation, resource reservations, and cancellation cascades.

Because a supervisor frequently manages children whose outputs depend causally on one another, runtime engines must maintain these three structures independently. Conflating message delivery with causal dependency or lifecycle ownership leads directly to race conditions, orphaned processes, and corrupted state.

18.3.1 Coordination arrangements

When migration_task_01 splits work across agents, the runtime must decide whether subtasks and candidate artifacts pass through a central coordinator, stream along staged queues, land in a shared store, or route directly between peer workers (table 2). The most prevalent pattern is the supervisor-worker topology (frequently termed orchestrator-worker). In this model, a centralized coordinator decomposes an incoming task, dispatches subtasks to subordinate workers, and synthesizes their returned receipts. This design directly mirrors the contract net protocol2 originally developed for distributed problem solving (Smith 1980). In production systems, these foundational topologies frequently compose. For example, a supervisor might dispatch parallel analytical investigations, stream their candidate patches through a staged validation pipeline, and persist intermediate progress within a shared task store.

2 Contract Net Protocol: In Smith’s 1980 protocol, a manager node announces a task, idle contractor nodes submit bids, and the manager awards the contract to a chosen bidder, which may subcontract in turn. The award represents the conceptual ancestor of the typed task envelope: it fixes which agent owns the subtask and what structured receipt must return, eliminating the ambiguity inherent in prompt-level delegation.

Each coordination topology makes different trade-offs regarding where state accumulates and where synchronization occurs. A supervisor concentrates integration logic and validation evidence at a single coordinator, requiring compact, structured receipts to prevent prompt context exhaustion. A pipeline structures execution as a sequence of discrete transformations, keeping each stage’s working context tightly focused while streaming items concurrently across stages. By contrast, a shared blackboard3 decouples agents entirely by externalizing coordination into a centralized artifact and task store (Erman et al. 1980). In an actor architecture, originally formulated by Hewitt, Bishop, and Steiger, agents maintain completely isolated private state and communicate exclusively via asynchronous message passing to explicit addresses (Hewitt et al. 1973). Both blackboards and actor meshes shift the coordination burden toward explicit conflict resolution, distributed ordering, and bounded mailbox management.

3 Blackboard Architecture: The Hearsay-II speech-understanding system of the 1970s coordinated independent knowledge sources that never communicated directly with one another, interacting solely by posting and reading hypotheses on a shared global data structure. Its central scheduling challenge, deciding which pending contribution to evaluate next, reappears whenever agent workers claim tasks from a shared operational store.

Message counts scale with the communication graph and its interaction protocol. If each of \(\WorkerCount\) participants transmits an update to every other participant during an execution round, the network must handle \(\WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1)\) directed deliveries. With an average payload size of \(\MeanDeliveryPayloadLength\) tokens per delivery, that single round consumes \(\WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1)\MeanDeliveryPayloadLength\) tokens across the system. In contrast, a supervisor managing \(\WorkerCount\) workers requires only \(2\WorkerCount\) deliveries per round (one dispatch envelope and one receipt per worker), consuming \(2\WorkerCount{}\MeanDeliveryPayloadLength\) tokens. The full mesh thus scales quadratically, transmitting \((\WorkerCount-1)/2\) times more messages per round than the centralized coordinator.

Raw transmission volume measures network traffic, not what the receiving models must ingest. In a fully connected mesh where each agent appends every incoming peer message to its prompt history, ingested tokens per round grow as \(\mathcal{O}(\WorkerCount^2)\). The append-only re-send of equation 2.9 compounds that growth across rounds, because each later prompt carries every earlier delivery. Even in a centralized topology, verbose receipts, iterative deliberation rounds, or frequent status polling against a blackboard store can exhaust context windows and inflate inference budgets. Across the four arrangements of figure 1, the supervisor’s round costs \(2\WorkerCount\) deliveries and the actor mesh up to \(\WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1)\), while the pipeline and blackboard replace direct deliveries with bounded queues and versioned claims that the runtime must police. An arrangement therefore keeps context windows bounded only when each handoff carries the specific inputs and checks a subtask requires, and deciding when those handoffs may fire belongs to the dependency graph.

18.3.2 Dependency graphs and concurrency

Dispatching subtasks before their prerequisites are verified wastes tokens on stale assumptions or stalls worker slots behind unfinished upstream steps. To schedule concurrent work safely, the runtime models task precedence as a directed acyclic graph (DAG), \((\DependencyGraphVertexSet,\DependencyGraphEdgeSet)\). Each vertex \(\DependencyGraphVertex \in \DependencyGraphVertexSet\) represents a bounded unit of execution, either an autonomous agent trajectory or a deterministic runtime validation check. A directed edge \((\PredecessorGraphVertex,\DependencyGraphVertex) \in \DependencyGraphEdgeSet\) indicates that vertex \(\DependencyGraphVertex\) requires a declared precondition satisfied by vertex \(\PredecessorGraphVertex\), such as an emitted code artifact, a validated test report, or an explicit authorization token. A status message signaling task completion satisfies an incoming edge only when accompanied by verifiable output receipts.

A vertex becomes dependency-ready once every prerequisite predecessor condition has been verified. Dependency readiness alone does not trigger execution, however. The runtime dispatcher must additionally verify execution authority, allocate an active worker slot, and secure a bounded token and financial budget reservation. If a predecessor fails or emits invalid artifacts, the control plane either halts downstream successors or transitions execution to an explicit recovery branch. Loops and iterative refinement cycles enter the graph as unrolled, bounded attempts. Because graph acyclicity guarantees only the absence of logical dependency loops, the underlying runtime remains responsible for detecting dynamic deadlocks arising from shared resource locks, exhausted thread pools, or saturated queues.

To estimate the theoretical limits of parallel scheduling, assume each vertex \(\DependencyGraphVertex\) occupies one interchangeable worker slot for a fixed duration \(\VertexSlotTime(\DependencyGraphVertex)\), encompassing both model inference latency and external tool execution. Let \(\WorkerCount\) represent the pool of available parallel worker slots. Under these assumptions, aggregate slot-work \(\AggregateSlotWork\) totals every vertex’s duration, and critical-path duration \(\CriticalPathDuration\) is the length of the longest dependency chain, the same critical path that trace analysis measures from recorded spans, here computed from assumed durations:

\[ \AggregateSlotWork=\sum_{\DependencyGraphVertex\in \DependencyGraphVertexSet}\VertexSlotTime(\DependencyGraphVertex),\qquad \CriticalPathDuration=\max_{\pi\in\operatorname{Paths}(\DependencyGraphVertexSet,\DependencyGraphEdgeSet)}\sum_{\DependencyGraphVertex\in\pi}\VertexSlotTime(\DependencyGraphVertex). \tag{2}\]

Any valid schedule’s total makespan \(\ScheduleFinishTime\) must satisfy the dual lower bounds

\[ \ScheduleFinishTime\ge\max\left(\frac{\AggregateSlotWork}{\WorkerCount},\CriticalPathDuration\right). \tag{3}\]

The aggregate work bound \(\AggregateSlotWork/\WorkerCount\) reflects the capacity constraint, since \(\WorkerCount\) parallel worker slots cannot complete \(\AggregateSlotWork\) total units of execution time in less than \(\AggregateSlotWork/\WorkerCount\), regardless of schedule structure. The critical-path bound \(\CriticalPathDuration\) reflects the causal precedence constraint, since sequential dependencies cannot be parallelized, and it establishes a lower bound on latency even with infinite worker slots.4 In production environments, resource contention, queuing overhead, payload serialization, and trajectory retries expand the overall makespan well beyond these theoretical minimums unless explicitly incorporated into vertex durations \(\VertexSlotTime(\DependencyGraphVertex)\) and graph edges.

4 Greedy List Scheduling: Graham established that any list schedule which never leaves a worker slot idle while a dependency-ready vertex waits completes within a factor of \(2-1/\WorkerCount\) of the optimal makespan. Graham also demonstrated that such schedules can suffer counterintuitive timing anomalies where adding worker slots or reducing individual task durations actually increases overall completion time (Graham 1969). A runtime dispatcher that greedily schedules ready work is therefore near-optimal relative to the theoretical bound in equation 3, and an observed latency regression after expanding worker pools does not necessarily indicate a scheduler implementation bug.

Graham, Ronald L. 1969. “Bounds on Multiprocessing Timing Anomalies.” SIAM Journal on Applied Mathematics 17 (2): 416–29. https://doi.org/10.1137/0117039.

↩︎ Trace-based measurement of the critical path is established in Span overlap and latency attribution.

Napkin Math 18.2: DAG scheduling in multi-agent execution
Problem: Consider an illustrative repository upgrade with 4 worker slots. Table 3 gives assumed durations for a single successful execution of each node. Each node occupies one slot; there are no additional dispatch delays, resource conflicts, retries, or transfer costs. How much can overlap?

Math: Summing the node durations as in equation 2 gives 275 s of aggregate slot-work. Dividing by the slot count gives the work term in equation 3, 68.75 s. The paths \(\DependencyGraphVertex_1\to \DependencyGraphVertex_2\to \DependencyGraphVertex_5\to \DependencyGraphVertex_7\) and \(\DependencyGraphVertex_1\to \DependencyGraphVertex_3\to \DependencyGraphVertex_5\to \DependencyGraphVertex_7\) tie for the critical path. The table’s schedule uses at most 3 slots, so it is feasible under the stated capacity and attains the path bound.

Result: Completion takes 174.5 s, a 1.58× speedup over executing these same nodes serially, and more slots cannot shorten the remaining chain.

Systems insight: Once a schedule attains the critical-path bound, as this one does, allocating worker slots beyond peak concurrency yields zero latency improvement. The off-critical branch through \(\DependencyGraphVertex_4\) and \(\DependencyGraphVertex_6\) finishes 26.5 s before \(\DependencyGraphVertex_7\) can start, meaning optimizations on that branch do not affect overall makespan. In contrast, shortening \(\DependencyGraphVertex_5\) or \(\DependencyGraphVertex_7\) yields a direct second-for-second reduction in total makespan up to the available slack. Shortening \(\DependencyGraphVertex_2\) in isolation provides no benefit because \(\DependencyGraphVertex_3\) ties it on the critical path. Analyzing production agent traces requires distinguishing a dependency-limited schedule like this one from a capacity-limited schedule, where additional slots or reduced contention can still shorten makespan.

18.3.3 Static plans and dynamic spawning

When a coordinator fixes every subtask before execution begins, workers cannot branch when they uncover an undocumented dependency. Conversely, letting a model spawn children at will can turn a simple query into dozens of redundant trajectories. A static plan resolves this trade-off toward predictability by specifying its stages, conditional branches, validation checkpoints, and retry bounds ahead of execution. Because all artifact dependencies and message routes are declared upfront, operators can inspect handoffs statically and verify safety invariants before dispatch. With enforced per-node work limits, bounded attempts, and stated service or deadline assumptions, the declared graph supports worst-case resource and execution bounds. A termination deadline does not guarantee accepted completion or immediate settlement of external work.

In contrast, dynamic spawning provisions child agents at runtime in response to intermediate observations. For example, during a repository migration, a worker analyzing call sites might uncover an undocumented dependency and request an isolated sub-investigation. While the model proposes this subtask dynamically, the runtime control plane must evaluate and admit it under the prevailing task contract. Dynamic spawning allows systems to adapt flexibly to open-ended workloads, but it introduces non-deterministic execution graphs. When an unconstrained model decides how many child agents to spawn and when to spawn them, combinatorial fan-out can quickly overwhelm system capacity and inflate execution costs.

War Story 18.1: Unbounded fan-out in a multi-agent research system (2025)
Context: Anthropic’s engineering team described building an autonomous research system where a lead orchestrator plans an investigation, spawns parallel subagents to search for evidence, and synthesizes their findings (Hadfield et al. 2025).

Mechanism: The lead agent possessed unconstrained authority to determine subagent fan-out. In early prototype versions, the model routinely spawned 50 subagents even for simple queries, searched endlessly for nonexistent sources, and allowed subagents to degrade coordination by flooding one another with excessive updates.

Impact: The engineering team reports that autonomous agents typically consume about 4× more tokens than standard conversational interactions, while multi-agent systems consume roughly 15× more. Although the multi-agent configuration outperformed a single agent by 90.2 percent on the team’s internal research evaluation, the unconstrained fan-out incurred disproportionate resource expenditure on straightforward tasks.

Fix: The team embedded explicit scaling heuristics into the orchestrator’s prompt, instructing the model to calibrate subagent allocation to query complexity, allocating a single agent for direct fact retrieval and scaling upward only for open-ended synthesis. Furthermore, the orchestrator was structured to wait synchronously for each dispatched subagent batch to conclude before initiating subsequent steps, simplifying state synchronization at the cost of pipeline parallelism.

Systems lesson: Dynamic fan-out is an architectural resource-allocation problem that cannot rely on model self-restraint alone. While prompt-based heuristics guide model proposals, the runtime control plane must enforce hard admission limits on tree breadth, recursion depth, and aggregate budget reservations. These limits directly bound the token consumption in the effective cost metric \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\).

Uncontrolled fan-out illustrates the necessity of formal admission control in multi-agent architectures. Prompt instructions alone cannot curb subtask generation, because an unconstrained model can still propose redundant branches or trigger cascading worker requests. Allocating each child a budget fraction smaller than the parent’s remaining funds also fails to protect the system, as a burst of concurrent siblings can issue overlapping claims that collectively exceed available capacity. The runtime admission controller must atomically reserve each child’s maximum potential liability against the shared task budget before dispatching execution, an invariant that section 18.8 formalizes across hierarchical delegation trees. Production runtimes must independently enforce four distinct bounds: fan-out breadth (maximum children per parent), concurrent active slots (maximum parallel executions), recursion depth (maximum delegation layers), and absolute deadlines. A shallow tree can still exhaust resources through excessive breadth, while a strict concurrency cap can still permit an unbounded sequence of sequential worker allocations.

↩︎ Bounded-liability admission, which reserves an operation’s maximum charge before admitting it, is established in Reservation and settlement.

18.3.4 Supervision trees

Beyond resource management, dispatching a child agent establishes lifecycle ownership. A child’s running trajectory, pending tool invocations, and uncommitted side effects typically outlive the ephemeral model call that spawned it. The supervision tree defines how the runtime isolates faults and manages recovery when a child encounters an unrecoverable failure. Following the supervision trees of Erlang/OTP (Open Telecom Platform),5 systems formalize recovery through explicit, per-supervisor restart strategies (Armstrong 2003, 2007):

5 Erlang Supervision Trees: Erlang emerged from a telephone-switching research program initiated in 1981, established on the design principle that systems should allow failing processes to crash cleanly while a designated supervisor restores known-good state, rather than attempting defensive error handling locally (Armstrong 2003). In agentic systems, this principle is even more vital: allowing a confused agent trajectory to attempt local recovery within a contaminated context window frequently compounds its errors, whereas a supervisor restart provisions a clean execution context from recorded inputs.

Armstrong, Joe. 2003. “Making Reliable Distributed Systems in the Presence of Software Errors.” PhD thesis, Royal Institute of Technology (KTH).
Armstrong, Joe. 2007. “A History of Erlang.” Proceedings of the Third ACM SIGPLAN Conference on History of Programming Languages (HOPL III), 6-1-6-26. https://doi.org/10.1145/1238844.1238850.
  • Under a one_for_one policy, the supervisor restarts only the failed child agent while allowing sibling workers to continue uninterrupted. This strategy is ideal for embarrassingly parallel or loosely coupled subtasks, such as searching disjoint documentation sources, where workers share no mutable intermediate state.
  • Under a one_for_all policy, the failure of any single child triggers the supervisor to terminate and restart all sibling workers within that supervision group. This approach is needed for tightly coupled collaborative tasks where partial artifacts become invalid upon peer failure. For example, if an interface worker crashes and restarts with an altered API contract, any partial implementation generated by a concurrent caller worker is invalidated.

The two policies differ in blast radius (figure 2): one failure under one_for_one costs a single fresh attempt and its reservation, whereas under one_for_all it discards the progress of every coupled sibling and restarts the whole group from recorded inputs. To prevent degenerate retry storms under either policy, supervision trees enforce a restart intensity bound, the maximum number of restarts permitted within a sliding time window. When a child agent repeatedly fails and breaches this threshold, the supervisor ceases local restarts, aborts its remaining subordinates, and escalates the failure upward to its own parent supervisor. In an agentic system, a restart must never continue from the failed worker’s corrupted or circular context window. Instead, the runtime treats each restart as a freshly admitted attempt. It provisions a clean context initialized from recorded input artifacts and acquires a fresh budget reservation. Whenever it aborts sibling processes under one_for_all or escalation, it also invokes the structured cancellation and cleanup protocols of section 18.7.

Checkpoint 18.1: Evaluating multi-agent coordination topologies

A coordination topology fixes how work, messages, and lifecycle ownership cross agent boundaries, but routing and supervision alone do not specify what a child is allowed to do once it starts. Without an explicit contract on each handoff, a worker can still read stale inputs, touch files outside its scope, or return a long narrative in place of verifiable evidence. Every boundary crossing therefore requires a formal task envelope that states what artifacts the worker receives, what tools and tokens it may consume, and what typed receipt it must return.

18.4 Typed Task Envelopes

A message asking worker_caller_01 to “update the callers” leaves critical engineering boundaries unspecified: the target interface version, the files permitted to change, the resource budget, and the acceptance criteria for completion. If the worker fills those gaps from its own interpretation of the prompt, that interpretation becomes the de facto contract. While a workflow dependency edge dictates when a child task may begin, the task envelope defines what that child is permitted to read, modify, spend, and return. Delegation therefore requires a typed action contract (principle 7) that extends across the agent boundary. It works much like a typed interface in a classic remote procedure call, which establishes formal argument types, return values, and failure semantics before execution begins (Birrell and Nelson 1984).

Definition 18.2: Typed task envelope

A typed task envelope is a versioned assignment record binding a child task to identified inputs, a scoped authority grant, resource and lifecycle limits, completion requirements, and a bounded return format.

  1. Significance: The runtime validates task boundaries before admitting work and verifies returned artifacts against required evidence. In addition, capping the return payload prevents runaway worker outputs from flooding the coordinator’s context window on subsequent execution turns.
  2. Distinction: The envelope defines an operational, enforceable contract for execution and resources; the natural-language prompt merely describes the objective within those constraints.
  3. Common pitfall: Mistaking a syntactically valid envelope for proof of authorization or successful execution. Both authorization and task completion require verification against authoritative state records maintained outside the worker’s control.

18.4.1 Task assignments

If a parent passes a subtask in free-form prose or embeds mutable budget numbers directly inside the child’s prompt, the child can misread its scope or rewrite its own limits. When delegating a subtask, the parent agent proposes the work, but the execution runtime authenticates the parent-child relationship, resolves the authority grant, and locks the resource reservation. In the task envelope shown in listing 1, field identifiers point to immutable registry records rather than embedding raw, unverified data. The referenced acceptance contract specifies expected caller behavior, exact test suite versions, the execution environment, and any required human reviews prior to final acceptance.

In listing 1, the task and dispatch identifiers tie the assignment to its durable write-ahead log intent, so that recovery can reconcile unresolved work before any permitted retry. The field base_ref pins the immutable repository snapshot exposed to the worker (any reads beyond that snapshot, such as queries to external package registries, require their own versioned references). Within that snapshot, grant_ref restricts the worker to editing designated caller files in an isolated scratch workspace, reading approved paths, and running local verification commands. The capability gate enforces parameter-level constraints on each tool invocation and explicitly withholds publication rights to shared branches. Alongside that authority grant, reservation_ref establishes hard limits on consumable resources, including input and output tokens as well as tool execution counts. Because the runtime resolves all three references against authoritative registry state before dispatch, a worker cannot expand its privileges or budget by modifying envelope fields in its own context.

↩︎ Durable intent, the admitted request recorded before dispatch so recovery retains its identity and can resolve an uncertain outcome, is established in Durable Intent Before Dispatch.

The remaining two blocks in listing 1 govern how the child returns its work. Completion requirements name the evidence records the runtime must collect, such as candidate diffs, dependency manifests, check reports, and usage and settlement records, before the task can count as complete. Return limits cap the receipt size, optional summary tokens, and artifact references so bulky build outputs stay in external storage rather than flooding the coordinator’s context window. The receipt described next records how the child met those rules.

18.4.2 Task receipts

When the child finishes or halts within those limits, the runtime packages the result into a receipt that mirrors the assignment envelope. The return payload in listing 2 describes a candidate change ready for integration review, one step short of an accepted modification. It strictly separates verified operational metadata from unverified model commentary. Runtime services directly populate and authenticate every field under runtime, whereas assertions generated by the worker model remain isolated under worker_claims.

The write-set record reflects the diff actually produced in the isolated workspace rather than the worker’s conversational recollection of what it modified. Similarly, the dependency record captures observed file reads, loaded package versions, configuration files, and external environment flags, bounded by explicit tracing coverage. The checker report records the exact candidate hash, the test command, the container environment, and the exit status. This record carries the evidence-scope rule of the tool boundary across the delegation boundary. A single call’s observation confirmed only what that call measured. A check result here establishes an outcome for one identified candidate hash, checker, and environment, and authorizes nothing beyond it. If the runtime cannot produce or verify every piece of mandatory evidence, the return status transitions to evidence_unavailable rather than complete.

↩︎ The evidence-scope rule, under which an observation confirms only what its tool measured during that invocation, is established in Complete mediation.

The usage record accounts for charged and reserved work across tokens, wall-clock time, and tool invocations. The settlement record distinguishes a stop acknowledgment, confirmed resource release, and unresolved background operations with retained owners. A complete return and the final settlement of every operation are therefore distinct statuses. This separation matters when the parent cancels an execution branch (section 18.7), ensuring that task termination does not leave orphaned background processes or dangling locks. Optional prose explanations may be shortened or omitted when space is constrained, with the omission explicitly recorded, while required identities, statuses, and evidence references pass intact. If the mandatory structural fields alone exceed receipt_bytes, the runtime generates an explicit payload-size failure rather than returning a truncated record.

18.4.3 Reference passing and selective reads

Instead of inlining an entire repository tree into a child task’s context prompt, the parent passes an immutable repository reference (base_ref). The worker then issues targeted read queries to fetch only the source files relevant to its assignment. However, this pass-by-reference pattern yields net token savings only when the child inspects a small fraction of the code base. If the worker iteratively fetches broad directory trees, the combined cost of reference overhead, tool invocation tokens, and repeated context re-reads across turns can quickly outstrip the cost of an upfront payload. Passing a shared base reference also amortizes KV-cache prefill across parallel workers, but only when their prompt prefixes align.

↩︎ Prompt prefix alignment and KV-cache prefill amortization are established in Preserving an exact prefix.

Napkin Math 18.3: Price the reads after the handoff
Problem: Each of 6 children receives either a 48,000-token repository slice or a 350-token reference envelope followed by 3,600 tokens of retrieved content. With all other inputs equal, no cache discount, and a hypothetical $3 per million input tokens, how much repository-delivery input does the reference save?

Math: Pasting costs 6 \(\times\) 48,000 \(=\) 288,000 tokens, or $0.864. The reference costs 6 \(\times\) (350 \(+\) 3,600) \(=\) 23,700 tokens, or $0.071, a 12.2× reduction. If every child instead reads the full slice, the reference design consumes 290,100 tokens and loses its saving.

Systems insight: The efficiency of reference passing depends entirely on read selectivity rather than the pointer mechanism alone. In this configuration, each child inspects only 7.5 percent of the slice, keeping reference passing economical until reads exceed 47,650 tokens. Effective multi-agent system design therefore pairs pointer handoffs with precise task scoping and indexing.

Once execution concludes, the receipt hands the parent coordinator an isolated candidate, its base version, and runtime-recorded evidence. Those records, not the absence of overlapping files, decide whether candidates can merge safely, because two patches that touch disjoint files can still break each other when integrated.

18.5 Optimistic Concurrency Control

In migration_task_01, both worker_interface_01 and worker_caller_01 branch from the same baseline commit, repo_base_01, to edit their respective files. When the interface worker finishes first, the integration service accepts its patch as repo_head_02. Because the caller worker modifies a different file, a standard three-way textual merge succeeds cleanly without merge conflicts. The caller’s patch, however, still targets the obsolete interface. Modifying separate files prevented a textual collision, but it left the semantic dependency behind the caller’s code unchecked.

Optimistic concurrency control (OCC) resolves this tension by letting each agent work privately against a snapshot and validating every publication prerequisite before the shared branch changes (Kung and Robinson 1981). A transaction runs a read phase against private copies, a validation phase that checks whether concurrent commits invalidated anything it read, and a write phase that commits only after validation succeeds. The expected-version check at the tool boundary is that validation applied to a single file. The scheme pays when conflicts are rare, because it trades lock waiting for occasional discarded work. A worker’s read phase, however, spans minutes of model generation and tool execution rather than the brief life of a database transaction, so a failed validation discards real inference spend. Its prerequisites include dependency freshness and acceptance evidence as well as unchanged files. Instead of holding a repository lock while an agent deliberates, the runtime decouples private execution from integration, which exposes parallelism and converts conflicts into the coordination tax of candidate revalidation and occasional discarded work.

↩︎ Conditional updates that commit only while the target still matches its expected version are established in Conditional updates.

18.5.1 Shared workspace hazards

Operating multiple agents within a single shared working directory introduces three immediate failure modes. A lost update occurs when two workers inspect the same file, deliberate over multiple inference turns, and serialize their writes back to disk. The later write silently replaces the earlier contribution, and the operating system reports no error because both writes were valid. A torn dependency read emerges when an agent executes tests while a peer is actively modifying related files, for example by evaluating code after a new interface has landed but before the caller has been updated. The test runner executes against an incoherent intermediate state that no agent ever intended to propose. Finally, tool-lock contention strikes when concurrent build tools or version-control commands collide on local metadata locks, such as Git’s .git/index.lock. One worker crashes on a locked index even when the underlying edits target entirely disjoint directories.

Shared writes pass even odds at five workers, long before a fleet feels large.

These collisions manifest surprisingly early as agent concurrency grows. Suppose a repository contains \(\RepositoryFileCount\) files and each of \(\WorkerCount\) workers modifies \(\WorkerWrittenFileCount\) distinct files, chosen uniformly and independently at random. Worker \(\EarlierWorkerCount+1\) avoids overlapping with all preceding workers only if it selects its \(\WorkerWrittenFileCount\) files exclusively from the \(\RepositoryFileCount-\EarlierWorkerCount \WorkerWrittenFileCount\) files that earlier workers left untouched. The probability that all \(\WorkerCount\) write sets remain pairwise disjoint is therefore

\[ \Pr(\text{no shared write})=\prod_{k=1}^{\WorkerCount-1}\frac{\binom{\RepositoryFileCount-\EarlierWorkerCount \WorkerWrittenFileCount}{\WorkerWrittenFileCount}}{\binom{\RepositoryFileCount}{\WorkerWrittenFileCount}}\approx\exp\left(-\frac{\WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1)\WorkerWrittenFileCount^2}{2\RepositoryFileCount}\right). \tag{4}\]

Equation 4 is the birthday bound6 generalized to multi-file write sets. When \(\WorkerWrittenFileCount\) is small relative to \(\RepositoryFileCount\), each of the \(\WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1)/2\) worker pairs compares \(\WorkerWrittenFileCount^2\) file choices against the \(\RepositoryFileCount\) repository files, so the exponent grows quadratically with both the worker count \(\WorkerCount\) and the write-set size \(\WorkerWrittenFileCount\). Because the exponent scales with the number of worker pairs rather than linearly with the worker count, collision odds mount rapidly. In a hypothetical repository of 1,000 files where each worker touches 10 files, even 2 concurrent workers collide with probability 9.6 percent. At 5 workers, a write collision becomes more likely than not, and scaling to 8 workers drives the collision probability to 94.4 percent. In practice, uniform random access is an illustrative baseline that can understate risk when writes cluster. Actual agent modifications heavily cluster around package manifests, central routing tables, and shared configuration files. Rigorous workspace isolation is therefore an architectural prerequisite for multi-agent coordination, rather than a performance tuning knob reserved for large worker pools.

6 Birthday Bound: The classical birthday problem calculates the number of people required before two share a birthday. The answer, twenty-three people for even odds across 365 days, often surprises newcomers because the number of potential pairs grows quadratically with group size. Systems designers exploit this same relation to size hash tables and cryptographic identifiers. In multi-agent execution, each worker represents an individual and the repository files represent the calendar, with collision probability amplified because each worker samples \(\WorkerWrittenFileCount\) distinct files simultaneously rather than a single day.

Providing each worker with an isolated workspace establishes a stable snapshot baseline and an unshared scratchpad for local modifications. When a worker finishes, the runtime therefore receives an immutable candidate artifact (identified by a cryptographic commit hash) rather than a reference to a mutable filesystem directory. Runtimes implement this isolation through two main mechanisms. Linked Git worktrees allocate a dedicated working tree, index, and HEAD pointer for each agent while sharing underlying object storage in .git/objects. Copy-on-write filesystems provide block-level cloning and zero-copy snapshots governed by runtime pooling lifecycles and reset contracts.

↩︎ Copy-on-write provisioning, which copies only what a task writes over a read-only base, is established in Copy-on-write provisioning.

Neither filesystem mechanism provides security on its own. If an agent executes arbitrary shell commands with unrestricted filesystem permissions, it can traverse parent directories, access peer worktrees, or corrupt the shared Git directory. A robust architecture therefore pairs workspace isolation with an external sandbox and a mediated tool gateway. The gateway confines filesystem writes to the assigned worktree, strips integration credentials, and allocates ephemeral ports and temporary directories for test suites. Furthermore, discarding a worktree cleanly recycles local ephemeral state, but it cannot revoke side effects that an external service has already accepted.

18.5.2 Dependency validation at integration

When a worker signals completion, the centralized integration service evaluates the returned execution receipt. The service resolves runtime.base_ref and runtime.candidate_ref, verifies cryptographic commit lineage, and independently derives the modified file paths rather than trusting a worker-reported write set. Beyond commit hashes, the service inspects runtime.dependencies_ref, which records the exact source versions, environmental flags, and file reads underpinning the candidate. Reliable dependency tracking requires runtime instrumentation of mediated filesystem calls, lockfiles, and configuration manifests; subjective, model-generated self-reports cannot be trusted for correctness. Furthermore, negative dependencies require careful tracking: an agent’s code generation may depend on the absence of a conflicting file, meaning that recording only positive file reads yields incomplete dependency graphs. Whenever dependency tracking is partial or ambiguous, the integration service treats an empty dependency manifest as unknown rather than disjoint, triggering a fresh revalidation of the candidate against the current branch head.

Let the commit content hashes \(\WorkerBaseCommitHash\), \(\WorkerCandidateCommitHash\), and \(\IntegrationHeadCommitHash\) identify the worker’s base, its returned candidate, and the integration head captured for this attempt. The protocol has four steps, which listing 3 condenses into the control flow of an integration service.

  1. Revalidate dependencies: Compare dependency versions between \(\WorkerBaseCommitHash\) and \(\IntegrationHeadCommitHash\). Pairwise disjoint file sets demonstrate only textual independence. If a referenced interface or schema changed on the main branch, the runtime must revise or regenerate the candidate before attempting integration.
  2. Build the candidate: Construct an immutable integration candidate \(\IntegrationCandidateCommitHash\). If \(\IntegrationHeadCommitHash\) still matches \(\WorkerBaseCommitHash\), the candidate is the worker’s own commit \(\WorkerCandidateCommitHash\) (the fast-forward path). If the head advanced, the service stages a private merge commit combining \(\IntegrationHeadCommitHash\) and \(\WorkerCandidateCommitHash\). Should textual or structural conflicts arise, the service may dispatch a bounded reconciliation task to an agent. The resulting merge artifact is treated as a fresh candidate proposal, subject to the full validation pipeline.
  3. Check the exact candidate: Every integration candidate must pass the scope and quality checks mandated by migration_accept_v1 within the protected assessment sandbox of Grader isolation. Under the evidence-scope rule, verification tests executed on the worker’s private branch do not validate the newly synthesized merge candidate. Acceptance criteria must be evaluated directly on the exact commit hash \(\IntegrationCandidateCommitHash\).
  4. Publish with an expected-head update: Advance the target ref if and only if the current branch pointer still equals the captured \(\IntegrationHeadCommitHash\) and all publication guards remain satisfied. These guards verify active grant authorization, unrevoked task status, comprehensive dependency tracking, and fresh acceptance test evidence. In Git, the low-level update-ref command with an expected old object hash guarantees an atomic compare-and-swap. The runtime sequences authorization guards with this atomic ref update to eliminate race conditions between token revocation and publication, while external dependencies are anchored by their action-time version contracts.

If a concurrent writer advances the branch head before the atomic ref update executes, the integration attempt aborts. Rather than blindly retrying the update against the new head, the service loops back to step 1, revalidating all dependencies against the updated state. If the publication command returns an ambiguous network outcome or timeout, the service must query the authoritative repository log before attempting a retry, preventing duplicate commits. Figure 3 assembles three ingredients: private worktrees over shared repository storage, a staged merge that is textually clean even though the caller relies on the interface as of \(\WorkerBaseCommitHash\), and the four-step gate whose moved-head exit returns to revalidation. Table 4 then follows the caller worker’s patch across each decision boundary.

Repeated invalidations quickly erode the efficiency gains of parallel delegation. If several agents continuously update overlapping dependencies, workers spend more compute discarding stale rollouts and reconciling candidates than generating productive code. Runtimes mitigate this through bounded retries, exponential backoff, and serialized commit queues, transitioning exhausted attempts into explicitly unresolved task states. However, when invalidation rates remain persistently high, the architectural remedy lies in work decomposition rather than concurrency tuning. Structuring the task graph to sequence interface migrations ahead of dependent caller tasks eliminates the cross-worker hazard entirely, achieving higher net throughput with fewer wasted agent turns.

18.5.3 Leases for external resources

Repository staging isolates and buffers only local repository state. A failed Git integration rolls back local commits, but it cannot undo mutations already executed on shared relational databases, physical test rigs, or external payment gateways. External resources operate under their own conditional-update and ownership semantics.

When managing unmergeable stateful resources (a live database migration lock, a dedicated test bench, or an embedded hardware debugger), the runtime must decide precisely when to enforce mutual exclusion. A pessimistic lease acquires an exclusive, time-bounded grant before an agent begins work, and the coordinator reclaims an expired grant so that a stalled holder cannot keep the resource indefinitely. Conflicting workers block until the lease holder releases the lock or times out, avoiding conflict-driven discarded work while the lease remains valid, but agent concurrency collapses into serialized execution. Conversely, optimistic control maximizes parallel execution by deferring conflict checks until publication. If validation fails, however, the runtime discards the entire execution trajectory, while any external side effects already dispatched to outside systems remain irreversible. This irreversibility governs the architectural division: optimistic concurrency control excels for private, discardable working trees, whereas the non-mergeable exclusive resources considered here require exclusion established at the target resource before issuing commands. Table 5 compares these operational strategies.

↩︎ Leases, the split-brain they permit when a stalled worker outlives its grant, and epoch fencing at the destination resource are established in Operation identity and endpoint contracts.

Checking lease validity inside the runtime before dispatching a command is not enough on its own, because an agent stalled by rate limits or slow inference can outlive its lease while a previously dispatched write is still in transit. Each leased resource therefore needs the resource-side fencing token of Availability and ownership, which stamps every lease transfer with a monotonically increasing generation number and rejects any write carrying an older generation. While the generation number fences out stale holders after a lease changes hands, a duplicate of the same operation sent by the current holder remains a separate hazard that the runtime settles through its operation identity under settlement before retry (principle 8). Delegation links these mechanisms whenever a supervisor restarts a failed or stalled worker under the policies of section 18.3.4, because the old attempt and the replacement attempt are siblings targeting the same external resource. Before the supervisor releases the replacement worker to issue commands, the runtime must advance the lease generation at the resource itself so that any late write from the superseded sibling is rejected at the destination.

↪ A delayed writer, traced before and after its replacement’s fencing token is installed, is developed in Leases and Fencing.

Guarded publication provides a robust concurrency framework for serializing concurrent state transitions. Its scope, however, is limited to structural and dependency consistency; OCC does not establish semantic correctness. If multiple workers inherit the same flawed assumptions from a shared planner, each worker can satisfy every step of the guarded publication protocol and cleanly commit a flawed patch to the repository.

18.6 Correlated Ensemble Failures

Adding a panel of five model reviewers to migration_task_01 to check semantic correctness before publication exposes the same shared-assumption hazard. All five reviewers may endorse worker_caller_01’s patch because they share the mistaken assumption that the legacy interface remains available, accumulating approvals without ever verifying the underlying dependency. This synchronized breakdown illustrates a correlated failure, in which the reviewers fail on the same inputs. When individual agents share systemic blind spots, consensus among them is informative only up to the limits of their joint error distribution.

18.6.1 Shared difficulty and conditional independence

Two classical foundations in fault tolerance appear to promise the opposite. Condorcet’s jury theorem (Condorcet, Marquis de 1785) states that if independent voters are each correct with probability above one half, the probability that a majority vote is correct approaches one as voters are added. In computer engineering, N-modular redundancy applies the identical arithmetic to hardware, masking a faulty replica through majority voting circuits (Neumann 1956). Both guarantees rest on a basic premise: that individual voters or components fail independently given the ground truth. Software engineering discovered during the 1980s that this premise breaks down in practice. Independently developed program versions failed on identical inputs far more frequently than statistical independence predicted7 (Knight and Leveson 1986). Table 6 contrasts the physical roots of independence in hardware redundancy with the fragile assumptions underlying model reviewer ensembles.

7 N-Version Programming: N-version programming builds several distinct implementations of a software specification and votes on their runtime outputs. Knight and Leveson had twenty-seven versions written independently across two universities and evaluated each against one million test inputs; although individual versions achieved high reliability, inputs on which several failed together were far more common than independence allowed (Knight and Leveson 1986). Language models trained on overlapping web-scale data face this exact vulnerability: benchmark evaluations across hundreds of models show heavily correlated errors, with the strongest correlation appearing among the largest and most capable models (Kim et al. 2025).

Knight, John C., and Nancy G. Leveson. 1986. “An Experimental Evaluation of the Assumption of Independence in Multiversion Programming.” IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering SE-12 (1): 96–109. https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.1986.6312924.
Kim, Elliot, Avi Garg, Kenny Peng, and Nikhil Garg. 2025. “Correlated Errors in Large Language Models.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML).

To quantify how correlation degrades redundancy, consider a binary review process evaluating candidate artifacts known to violate an acceptance criterion. Let \(\ReviewerFalseApprovalIndicator_j=1\) denote the event where reviewer \(\ReviewerIndex\) erroneously approves an invalid candidate, with marginal false-approval probability \(\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability\) over this candidate population. If an ensemble of \(\WorkerCount\) reviewers makes independent errors with identical probability \(\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability\), their total number of false approvals \(\FalseApprovalCount\) follows a binomial distribution. For an odd ensemble size \(\WorkerCount\), a faulty majority forms with probability

\[ \Pr(\FalseApprovalCount>\WorkerCount/2)=\sum_{k=(\WorkerCount+1)/2}^{\WorkerCount}\binom{\WorkerCount}{\FalseApprovalOutcomeCount}\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability^\FalseApprovalOutcomeCount(1-\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability)^{\WorkerCount-\FalseApprovalOutcomeCount}. \]

Statistical independence must always be stated relative to the information held fixed. Repeated, independently sampled calls to a single model on one fixed input prompt can be conditionally independent. However, candidate defects vary widely in real workloads: some bugs are subtle and intricate, whereas others are straightforward. A challenging candidate is difficult for every reviewer. Across the broader candidate population, individual reviewer errors become positively correlated. Furthermore, multi-agent debate protocols introduce an intentional communication channel where agents revise their answers after inspecting peer conclusions (Du et al. 2023). This deliberate coupling introduces a secondary layer of dependence on top of shared task difficulty.

A latent-propensity model makes this shared difficulty explicit. Eckhardt and Lee originally introduced this framework to explain coincident software failures (Eckhardt and Lee 1985), assigning each candidate input an intrinsic propensity \(\FalseApprovalPropensity \in [0, 1]\) of causing a randomly chosen version to fail. In an agent review setting, draw a candidate with false-approval propensity \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\). Conditional on \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\), each reviewer independently issues an erroneous approval with probability \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\). Reviewers share the candidate’s intrinsic difficulty, even though their individual sampling realizations remain independent. On a candidate of propensity \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\), two distinct reviewers therefore err together with probability \(\FalseApprovalPropensity^2\). Averaging across the candidate population gives a marginal false-approval rate of \(\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability=\mathbb{E}[\FalseApprovalPropensity]\) and a joint error probability of \(\mathbb{E}[\FalseApprovalPropensity^2]\), so subtracting \(\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability^2\) leaves a pairwise covariance equal to \(\operatorname{Var}(\FalseApprovalPropensity)\), which divides by the marginal Bernoulli variance \(\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability(1-\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability)\) to give the correlation coefficient \(\CorrelationCoefficient\):

\[ \MarginalFalseApprovalProbability=\mathbb{E}[\FalseApprovalPropensity],\qquad \operatorname{Cov}(\ReviewerFalseApprovalIndicator_i,\ReviewerFalseApprovalIndicator_j)=\operatorname{Var}(\FalseApprovalPropensity),\qquad \CorrelationCoefficient=\frac{\operatorname{Var}(\FalseApprovalPropensity)}{\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability(1-\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability)}\quad(\PeerReviewerIndex\ne \ReviewerIndex). \]

To model this variation analytically, assume \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\sim\operatorname{Beta}(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape,\PropensityPriorRejectionShape)\), a flexible density on \([0,1]\) with positive shape parameters \(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape\) and \(\PropensityPriorRejectionShape\). Its mean is \(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape/(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape+\PropensityPriorRejectionShape)\), and for reviewers who share \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\) the pairwise correlation works out to \(1/(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape+\PropensityPriorRejectionShape+1)\), so a small \(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape+\PropensityPriorRejectionShape\) means candidates differ widely in difficulty and errors correlate strongly. Setting the mean to the marginal rate and the correlation to the target gives \(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape+\PropensityPriorRejectionShape=\CorrelationCoefficient^{-1}-1\), which implies \(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape=\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability(\CorrelationCoefficient^{-1}-1)\) and \(\PropensityPriorRejectionShape=(1-\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability)(\CorrelationCoefficient^{-1}-1)\) for \(0<\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability<1\) and \(0<\CorrelationCoefficient<1\). Compounding the conditional binomial distribution over this beta prior yields the beta-binomial probability for \(\FalseApprovalOutcomeCount\) false approvals:

\[ \Pr(\FalseApprovalCount=\FalseApprovalOutcomeCount)=\binom{\WorkerCount}{\FalseApprovalOutcomeCount}\frac{\mathrm{B}(\FalseApprovalOutcomeCount+\PropensityPriorApprovalShape,\WorkerCount-\FalseApprovalOutcomeCount+\PropensityPriorRejectionShape)}{\mathrm{B}(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape,\PropensityPriorRejectionShape)}, \]

where \(\mathrm{B}\) denotes the beta function. A stipulated marginal error rate and pairwise correlation do not uniquely determine the full joint distribution, and the beta family represents an idealized model for shared difficulty. Consequently, any production deployment must empirically measure its joint error distribution directly on domain-specific candidates.

↪ The derivation of the beta-binomial false-approval count from the latent-propensity model is developed in Correlated failures in multi-agent quorum voting.

Napkin Math 18.4: What the joint error model changes
Problem: Five reviewers assess known-invalid candidates, each with marginal false-approval probability \(\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability=0.20\). How often does a majority falsely approve if their errors are independent, and how often under the beta mixture at \(\CorrelationCoefficient=0.40\)?

Math: Summing the binomial masses for three or more false approvals gives 5.79 percent under independent errors. The beta-binomial masses with \(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape=\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability(\CorrelationCoefficient^{-1}-1)\) and \(\PropensityPriorRejectionShape=(1-\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability)(\CorrelationCoefficient^{-1}-1)\) give 16.68 percent. Unanimous false approval changes from 0.032 percent to 3.92 percent.

Systems insight: At the same marginal error rate, positive correlation erases most of the reliability gains expected from voting. The mixture’s majority improves only slightly on a single reviewer’s marginal rate \(\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability\), and a unanimous false approval is about 122 times as likely as independence predicts. Before adding reviewers to an ensemble, estimate \(\CorrelationCoefficient\) empirically on known-invalid candidates. When correlation is high, spending additional token budget on more reviewers of the same type yields diminishing returns. That budget is better spent on independent, deterministic validation checks. These calculations condition on the candidate being invalid; the overall probability that an approved candidate is invalid also depends on the prevalence of invalid candidates in the workload.

The mixture model also bounds what ensemble scaling can achieve. Conditional on a candidate’s propensity \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\), the weak law of large numbers implies that the fraction of reviewers falsely approving converges to \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\) as the ensemble grows. Consequently, an arbitrarily large majority vote falsely approves precisely those candidates with \(\FalseApprovalPropensity>1/2\). Rather than decaying toward zero, majority false approval approaches a nonzero asymptotic floor at \(\Pr(\FalseApprovalPropensity>1/2)\).

Under the parameters modeled in the notebook, that floor is 15.0 percent. At 15 reviewers, independent errors would reduce majority false approvals to 0.42 percent, whereas the beta mixture leaves the error rate stalled at 15.6 percent. In figure 4 the independent curve keeps falling toward zero while the mixture curve flattens just above the floor. At five reviewers the mixture moves probability to the extremes, so a candidate is approved by no one or by a majority far more often than independence predicts. Tripling the committee from five to 15 reviewers of the same kind triples review spend yet lowers majority false approval only from 16.68 percent to 15.6 percent, because the remaining errors concentrate on candidates that every such model finds difficult.

18.6.2 Evidence at the integration boundary

In an automated workflow such as migration_task_01, five reviewers that all repeat the assumption that the old interface remains available provide essentially a single effective observation. Their consensus should not trigger an automatic merge; instead, it should trigger an end-to-end integration check that evaluates the modified caller alongside the updated callee interface. A protected, deterministic check supplies ground-truth evidence that model reviewers cannot fabricate. A compiler establishes whether the candidate compiles cleanly under the target configuration, and a regression test suite confirms that specified functional behaviors remain intact. If an untrusted worker agent modifies a test suite to bypass a signature verification check, the test may pass while completely failing to validate the security property. This vulnerability is why authoritative evaluation suites must remain isolated behind a protected grading boundary. Even so, deterministic validation remains bounded by test suite coverage gaps and flawed formal specifications.

Classical distributed quorum protocols cannot rescue a review ensemble plagued by correlated errors. When a large language model (LLM) outputs well-formed, plausible, yet incorrect evidence, it produces a fail-plausible fault rather than a fail-stop crash. In the unsigned-message protocol model, Byzantine agreement guarantees consensus despite arbitrary faults only while faulty participants remain strictly below one third of the ensemble8 (Lamport et al. 1982). Because reviewers that share training distributions, system prompts, or upstream reasoning summaries fail together, correlated errors can push the faulty fraction past that one-third threshold regardless of replica count. Requiring each reviewer to inspect primary evidence before viewing peer conclusions (information isolation) removes direct peer influence. Distinct model architectures, diversified prompts, and separate evidence sources lower \(\CorrelationCoefficient\) only as far as empirical measurement on known-invalid candidates demonstrates.

8 Byzantine Bound: The replica bound fixes how large a faulty fraction a protocol tolerates, not why replicas fail; the one-third limit applies to unsigned messages, and digital signatures relax it (Lamport et al. 1982). Classical deployments seek to keep simultaneous faults below that bound through physical diversity, running distinct code bases on separate hardware in different administrative domains. Model reviewers drawn from common model families, or trained on overlapping web-scale data, lack that isolation by default.

Lamport, Leslie, Robert Shostak, and Marshall Pease. 1982. “The Byzantine Generals Problem.” ACM Transactions on Programming Languages and Systems 4 (3): 382–401. https://doi.org/10.1145/357172.357176.

↩︎ The fail-plausible fault, output that is well formed and plausible yet wrong, is established in The fail-plausible fault.

Consensus among worker agents must never be confused with authoritative execution privilege. Reviewer agreement provides an informative heuristic signal within the bounds defined by an orchestration workflow.
