Merging code, publishing artifacts, broadening task scopes, or bypassing gate checks requires explicit capability grants issued by a privileged control plane. Even unanimous approval from the five reviewers in migration_task_01 only advances the candidate to the integration gate, where the runtime still checks its scope, dependencies, and publication grant before updating the branch.

Checkpoint 18.2: Interpreting concurrent results and agreement

Validation evidence also carries a finite lifetime while asynchronous workers execute in parallel. If an upstream orchestrator rejects an intermediate candidate patch or alters its task plan, downstream worker agents may continue computing on stale state. Robust agent runtimes must invalidate and reject superseded work while properly terminating and accounting for in-flight child processes.

18.7 Bounding In-Flight Work

Suppose the supervisor cancels migration_task_01 while worker_interface_01 awaits an external test job and worker_caller_01 finishes generating a candidate patch. Even if the caller’s patch passes its local validation checks after the supervisor initiates cancellation, the runtime must ensure that the patch remains strictly unpublishable. Meanwhile, an external testing service may have already accepted the interface worker’s test request, incurring resource and billing charges that remain unsettled long after the worker process halts. In single-agent execution, a cancellation signal interrupts a local loop or an outstanding remote inference call. In a multi-agent system, that control boundary must span an entire hierarchy of distributed descendants.

↩︎ Pause and cancellation of a single trajectory, applied at turn boundaries and by streaming cancellation, are established in Control Actions.

To enforce cancellation across a fleet of workers, the runtime records the affected subtree, its current control generation, and the rationale for stopping. Authoritative dispatch and publication gates consult this record along the serialized path that admits new work. In distributed agent systems, closing admission, terminating execution, and releasing resources are three distinct milestones, each demanding its own independent evidence. Everything admitted before the cancellation stays under the settlement rule of failure recovery, now applied across the entire subtree. An operation admitted before the stop preserves its identity, execution status, and assigned owner until its outcome and associated financial charges settle. No cancellation signal, authorization revocation, timeout, or queue purge can retroactively erase that obligation.

↩︎ The settlement rule, which keeps an operation’s reservation and record until evidence establishes its outcome, is established in Effects Outside a Shared Transaction.

18.7.1 Cancellation context trees

When a parent task is canceled, a grandchild worker that has not yet received the stop signal can keep calling tools and publishing results under the parent’s delegated authority. The cancellation context tree prevents that leak without introducing a fourth graph structure. It is the supervision tree of section 18.3.4 seen from the control plane, with the same parent-child ownership edges, each annotated with the control generation of the ancestor that governs the child. A child’s delegation lineage is its path from that tree’s root. Cancellation proceeds in two steps across the runtime’s graphs. First, the runtime traces delegation lineage rather than data-dependency edges to determine which active workers must lose their authority and budget. Because execution mandates and resource allowances flow down the supervision tree, revoking an ancestor immediately strips every descendant in that subtree of eligibility to run tools or publish results. Second, once that subtree is marked ineligible, the runtime inspects the data-dependency graph to see which tasks outside the canceled subtree were waiting on its outputs. Any downstream continuation that required an artifact from the canceled subtree can no longer satisfy its preconditions and is invalidated, whereas sibling or downstream tasks that treated the canceled child as an optional speculative branch continue undisturbed.

Definition 18.3: Hierarchical cancellation context tree

Hierarchical cancellation context tree is a runtime-owned delegation structure that links each child to the ancestor control decisions governing its continued execution and result publication.

  1. Significance: It identifies which descendants must lose eligibility when an ancestor stops and which outstanding operations still need an owner.
  2. Distinction: Propagating a stop request changes control policy; confirming remote termination, resource release, and effect settlement requires separate evidence.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating a canceled parent record as proof that every child, external job, and charge has ended.

The ordering of events at each admission and publication gate dictates the precise boundary of cancellation. Suppose an admission gateway accepts worker_interface_01’s test operation, logs its unique operation identity along with its resource reservation, and begins transmitting the request. If the parent task cancels before a reply arrives, that test operation remains admitted and must proceed through the settlement protocol. However, any subsequent request from either child that arrives at that gateway after the cancellation decision is serialized will be rejected immediately. Result publication follows the exact same linear order. The integration path must re-verify task eligibility at the atomic publication boundary: if the cancellation commits first, it blocks publication of any patch, regardless of whether that patch passed every local check. If publication commits before the cancellation is serialized at the boundary, the resulting state change stands as a durable modification that the recovery contract must handle.

Under this architecture, a supervisor can authoritatively report that its gateways deny new work and reject publications without falsely claiming that a disconnected or remote worker process has already halted. A remote enforcement gateway that loses connectivity to the central control plane must fail closed. If a gateway instead relies on cached authorization tokens, it operates under an explicit stale-authorization window and cannot guarantee immediate, subtree-wide revocation (section 18.8).

18.7.2 Evidence preservation during cleanup

Closing a local client stream does not confirm that a remote model provider has ceased generation, and killing a local worker process leaves requests accepted by external services in flight. Because local termination and remote completion are asymmetric across distributed boundaries, the runtime must dispatch explicit stop requests to each reachable execution service, with each acknowledgment certifying only its specific operational scope (table 7). An executor’s process-level or infrastructure confirmation always outranks an agent’s self-reported claim that it has halted.

For an outstanding test request, the runtime retains the operation identity, execution attempt history, resource reservation, and external job handle while actively reconciling status. If the external test service eventually delivers a late result, that response can settle the operation’s outcome and finalize its billing without reactivating the canceled task trajectory. If the external outcome remains indeterminate, the runtime transfers the operation to a designated settlement owner. The execution receipt preserves these audit records through runtime.settlement_ref, ensuring they survive any context-window summarization or lineage compaction.

Cleanup operations cannot execute under the revoked worker’s authority; the runtime must provision a separately scoped administrative credential and a protected resource allowance. Furthermore, tearing down an allocated environment is itself an asynchronous operation with an uncertain outcome. Scratch workspace or container deletion must block until all relevant filesystem writers have terminated and required diagnostic artifacts have been safely archived. An asynchronous background sweeper reconciles registered physical allocations against these pending evidentiary obligations before unlinking directories or recycling sandbox containers.

↩︎ The cleanup graph that orders this teardown is established in The cleanup graph.

18.7.3 Hedged requests and speculative work

Waiting for every worker drops on-time odds below half by seven workers.

Beyond handling failures and user aborts, cancellation is a primitive for speculative execution and latency management. When an agent workflow fans out across multiple subtasks, waiting for every parallel worker exposes the entire system to stragglers, the tail-at-scale problem (Dean and Barroso 2013). Consider \(\WorkerCount\) parallel workers with independent and identically distributed completion times \(\WorkerCompletionTime_i\) required to produce an admissible result. If \(\WorkerCompletionDistribution(\TimeInstant)=\Pr(\WorkerCompletionTime_i\le \TimeInstant)\) represents the cumulative probability of a single worker finishing within time \(\TimeInstant\), an all-results barrier, which requires every worker to complete, achieves a deadline completion probability of

\[\Pr\bigl(\max_i \WorkerCompletionTime_i\le \TimeInstant\bigr)=\WorkerCompletionDistribution(\TimeInstant)^{\WorkerCount}.\]

Because the barrier needs every worker’s admissible result, each added worker multiplies the deadline probability by another factor \(\WorkerCompletionDistribution(\TimeInstant)<1\), so it decays geometrically as the fan-out \(\WorkerCount\) grows. This is the same geometric decay that horizon compounding (equation 1.1) applies across turns, now applied across workers. In production systems, correlated queueing delays, shared rate limits, and unequal prompt lengths break the idealized independent model, so deadline probabilities must be measured under the shared serving conditions. However, if the task contract permits any \(\RequiredResultCount\) interchangeable admissible results out of \(\WorkerCount\) replicas, the probability of meeting the deadline becomes

\[\Pr(\WorkerCompletionTime_{(k)}\le \TimeInstant)=\sum_{j=k}^{\WorkerCount}\binom{\WorkerCount}{\FinishedWorkerCount} \WorkerCompletionDistribution(\TimeInstant)^\FinishedWorkerCount[1-\WorkerCompletionDistribution(\TimeInstant)]^{\WorkerCount-\FinishedWorkerCount},\]

where \(\WorkerCompletionTime_{(k)}\) denotes the arrival time of the \(\RequiredResultCount\)th admissible result. This \(\RequiredResultCount\)-of-\(\WorkerCount\) stopping rule applies strictly to interchangeable outputs, such as parallel candidate retrieval or redundant reasoning paths. In the migration scenario, by contrast, the system requires both the interface update and the caller refactor; no majority of completed caller patches can stand in for a missing interface patch.

Napkin Math 18.5: A completion contract changes the barrier
Problem: A hypothetical read-only analysis task permits any 6 interchangeable admissible results from 8 workers. Each worker independently has a 90 percent probability of returning such a result within 10 s of dispatch, including its checks. What is the probability of having sufficient results by that deadline, compared with waiting for every worker?

Math: Requiring every result gives \(\WorkerCompletionDistribution(\TimeInstant)^{\WorkerCount}\), or 43.05 percent. Summing the binomial probabilities for 6 through 8 completions gives 96.19 percent.

Systems insight: An all-results barrier multiplies the deadline probability by \(\WorkerCompletionDistribution(\TimeInstant)\) for every worker added; at this per-worker rate it drops below even odds at 7 workers. Where the contract permits interchangeable results, a \(\RequiredResultCount\)-of-\(\WorkerCount\) stopping rule recovers most of the lost probability. Where it does not, the fan-out itself must shrink or the per-worker tail must be cut. The workers still running after the rule fires consume resources until cancellation takes effect, and that work belongs in the cost account.

Hedging9 mitigates tail latency by dispatching a redundant replacement attempt after a specified delay while the original worker continues running, subsequently accepting whichever admissible result completes first (Dean and Barroso 2013). Hedging functions smoothly for read-only analyses and sandboxed candidate patches where a single runtime publication gate arbitrates the winner and cancels redundant runners. However, speculative hedging introduces acute hazards if an agent executes external mutating actions. Unless the destination service implements a strict duplicate-effect or idempotency contract, a trailing or canceled worker can execute irreversible side effects before its cancellation signal is processed. A hedge of a mutating action is a retry issued before the first attempt has settled, which settlement before retry (principle 8) forbids unless both attempts carry one operation identity that the endpoint deduplicates. Furthermore, speculative attempts consume real token allowances and concurrency slots; dispatching hedges into a congested inference cluster amplifies queue depth and prolongs the very tail latencies the mechanism was intended to truncate.

9 Hedged Requests: Dean and Barroso’s recipe defers the second request until the first has been outstanding longer than the 95th-percentile expected latency, which bounds the extra load to roughly the fraction of requests that are that slow. For agents the delay matters more than for storage reads, because each hedge is a full model trajectory with its own reservation, and a hedge issued too early doubles spend on the common case to shorten the rare one.

Dean, Jeffrey, and Luiz André Barroso. 2013. “The Tail at Scale.” Communications of the ACM 56 (2): 74–80.

↩︎ Uncertain outcomes and the endpoint deduplication contracts that make a repeated mutation safe are established in Retrying Uncertain Actions.

18.7.4 Credit-based flow control

Even when every child agent stays within its own token budget, a rate mismatch between stages of a pipeline can still destabilize the workflow. A fast producer that emits candidate changes faster than a downstream reviewer or test runner can drain them fills the queue between the two stages. As that backlog grows, candidates wait longer before inspection, so the repository state they were built against moves underneath them while they sit in line.

Consider an upstream agent that lists files and emits candidate diffs at \(\ArrivalRate =\) 5 items per second. A downstream reviewer model generates a 500-token critique for each item at a decode speed of 40 tokens per second, yielding an effective service rate of only \(\ServiceRate =\) 0.08 items per second. In an unconstrained queue, the backlog expands at the net rate \(\ArrivalRate - \ServiceRate\). After 10 minutes, the queue accumulates 2,952 unreviewed items, and a newly submitted diff faces an estimated queue wait of 10.2 hours. Long before process memory is exhausted, the queued items become obsolete, because the repository state they reference has diverged, reproducing between agents the stale-observation failure that context invalidation guards against inside one trajectory. Per-agent token budgets fail to prevent this collapse because each individual candidate item is inexpensive to generate.

↩︎ Context invalidation, which withdraws observations that a later change has made stale, is established in Context Invalidation.

A finite credit window prevents this divergence by bounding the number of admitted, in-flight items, much as an aggregate budget caps token expenditure. It is the same mechanism that bounds staged records in trajectory collection. A producer transmits only while it holds a credit the consumer issued for free capacity, so congestion delays the producer but never overflows a buffer. Let \(\CreditWindowCapacity\) denote the fixed credit capacity of the channel, \(\FreeCreditCount\) the unallocated credits held by the producer, and \(\OutstandingItemCount\) the outstanding admitted items currently circulating in the pipeline. The runtime enforces the structural invariant:

\[\FreeCreditCount+\OutstandingItemCount=\CreditWindowCapacity,\qquad \FreeCreditCount\ge0,\quad \OutstandingItemCount\ge0.\]

↩︎ Collection credits, which bound staged trajectories between generation and verification, are established in Collection credits and staging reserves.

Under this discipline, admitting a new work item requires the producer to acquire and attach an available credit. That credit remains tied to the item while it traverses the network, waits in downstream queues, undergoes evaluation, or reserves output buffer space. The runtime returns the credit to the producer’s pool only when the item reaches a designated capacity-release boundary. Credit returns are processed atomically and with at-most-once semantics. A lost return silently shrinks the window, and a duplicated one inflates it, so network disconnections trigger a recount of outstanding credits against the authoritative runtime state rather than granting producers a fresh batch of credits.

Beyond protecting buffer memory, credit windows provide a sizing model for mean residence at a sustained service rate. Little’s law (theorem 5.1, equation 5.7) states that the mean number of items held equals their admitted rate times their mean residence. Applying it to a saturated window takes one mapping. The items held are the outstanding ones, \(\OutstandingItemCount=\CreditWindowCapacity\). The window admits a new item only when the consumer drains one, so the admitted rate is the drain rate \(\ServiceRate\), not the producer’s offered rate \(\ArrivalRate\). Dividing the items held by the admitted rate gives a mean residence of \(\CreditWindowCapacity/\ServiceRate\). With a window of \(\CreditWindowCapacity =\) 8 credits in the pipeline above, a diff that would have waited 10.2 hours behind the unbounded backlog instead resides in the window for 100 seconds on average. A hard freshness requirement additionally needs admission timestamps, deadline checks before service, and bounded service times if timely service must be guaranteed. When credits are exhausted, the producer yields execution instead of flooding the pipeline with artifacts destined to be obsolete upon inspection. Furthermore, if each in-flight payload is constrained to at most \(\DataVolume\) bytes, total memory occupancy cannot exceed \(\CreditWindowCapacity\DataVolume\). This demonstrates why payload return bounds (section 18.4) serve as admission-control parameters rather than mere prompt formatting conventions.

Exhausting data credits, however, must never starve control-plane traffic. Signals such as cancellation commands, credit reconciliation messages, execution status receipts, and financial settlement records travel over reserved out-of-band control channels. The runtime must reject new task admissions whenever doing so would risk exhausting the execution slots reserved for downstream consumers and cleanup handlers. Otherwise, upstream producers waiting for credits will block the very consumers needed to release them, deadlocking the system. Finally, releasing a channel credit must not be conflated with releasing an external resource or refunding a financial commitment. Credit reclamation restores pipeline capacity, whereas financial settlement accounts for external cost.

Together, cancellation trees and credit windows bound the volume and financial exposure of in-flight work while keeping control paths responsive, and the credit window sets mean residence under the stated service rate. Neither control, however, decides which operations a child is allowed to propose while it runs. Enforcing those limits across a delegation tree depends on the authority model at each node: what capabilities each descendant holds, how its allowance is reserved, and how fresh its lineage must be when checked at a remote gateway (section 18.8).

18.8 Capability Delegation

Suppose worker_caller_01, an agent authorized only to update selected caller functions, spawns a new child task to inspect upstream source code and designates the root orchestrator as that child’s parent. If the runtime naively resolves authority from the client-supplied parent identifier, the inspection child inherits the root’s global authority, including publication credentials intended exclusively for the release integration service. The delegation tree (the supervision tree’s parent-child edges, along which grants now flow) has itself manufactured a confused deputy (definition 7.3). The runtime resolved authority from a name the requester supplied, the parent identifier that the child chose for itself. Similarly, two sibling workers dispatched concurrently might both observe the parent’s uncommitted budget before either issues an external call, overdrawing the task’s shared financial or token allowance. Task cancellation exposes a matching temporal hazard: a descendant grant that was perfectly valid when minted must admit no further work once the runtime revokes an ancestor in its lineage.

Scoped grants and credential brokering establish where authority resides within isolated execution environments. Multi-agent architectures extend this foundation through attenuated delegation: every hop down the task hierarchy passes authority no broader than the delegator’s existing authority. This model enforces the principle of least privilege (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975) with capabilities (definition 8.2). The authority therefore travels in the reference a child holds rather than being looked up from the identity it claims, and confinement survives concurrent spending and ancestor state transitions. While a model may propose spawning a child task and suggest its scope, the runtime supervisor alone assigns the child’s identity, issues its attenuated grant, commits its resource reservation, and binds its lifecycle.

Saltzer, J. H., and M. D. Schroeder. 1975. “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems.” Proceedings of the IEEE 63 (9): 1278–308. https://doi.org/10.1109/proc.1975.9939.

18.8.1 Attenuated grants and split allowances

Passing a parent’s full credential or unreserved budget balance to a child lets a single compromised subtask modify unrelated files or spend the entire task allowance. To keep each hop narrower than the one above it, a delegation tree applies the containment relation \(\GrantAllowedAccesses(\ChildCapabilityGrant)\subseteq\GrantAllowedAccesses(\ParentCapabilityGrant)\) established in Narrowing delegated authority at every hop. In a multi-agent tree the relation governs concrete operations executed on underlying system resources, rather than superficial labels such as tool names or directory prefixes that an agent might attempt to manipulate. For example, an inspection child task may hold permission to inspect a specific interface file, but it must possess no rights to modify caller source files, exfiltrate repository data, or access the parent broker’s credentials for external repositories. The execution boundary enforces these perimeter restrictions directly; the child environment starts without inheriting broader host credentials or open file handles.

Runtimes can implement these bindings using either protected internal kernel handles or cryptographically verifiable bearer tokens. Chained-caveat tokens let a holder attenuate its grant locally without contacting an issuer, because each added restriction extends a keyed hash chain that the holder cannot unwind (Birgisson et al. 2014). Because the runtime supervisor, never the generative model, binds the resulting capability to the specific task execution context, a compromised or malfunctioning worker cannot forge a parent lineage or substitute a token belonging to another task.

Permissions and consumable budgets require different delegation rules. Permissions are not depleted by use, so they can be shared: a parent that delegates read access retains its own ability to read. Consumable budgets, by contrast, are depletable resources that must be partitioned rather than duplicated, ensuring that each unit of currency or token quota is spent at most once. The admission control gate must therefore ensure that no unit of budget is created from nothing or spent twice anywhere in the delegation hierarchy. Before dispatching a child task, the runtime atomically reserves the child’s admitted liability against the shared allowance, accounting for all concurrent siblings and the parent’s own outstanding work. This atomic reservation prevents two sibling workers from racing to commit the same remaining balance. It is the shared-allowance rule of trajectory-level encapsulation (principle 10) applied across a tree, under which concurrent holders reserve against one allowance and a derived allowance only narrows. If a child subsequently subdivides its allowance among its descendants, it creates a nested sub-budget that can never exceed its own allocation, not an independent pool that can be charged separately.

↪ The settlement rules developed in Budget conservation across descendants decide when a delegated allocation’s unspent backing returns to the parent and count a late bill once.

18.8.2 Revocation and lineage checks

In a distributed multi-agent system, revocation cannot depend on promptly delivering push notifications to every running child. A robust architectural pattern assigns each task node in the runtime a monotonic generation counter and an operational state (such as active, canceled, or completed). When a grant is issued, the runtime binds it cryptographically to the complete root-to-holder lineage path, recording the exact generation counter of each ancestor at the moment of delegation. Revoking an ancestor marks that node ineligible or advances its generation counter. Node restarts must monotonically advance or preserve generation counters rather than resetting to zero, which would inadvertently revive expired or revoked grants.

When an agent submits an operation to the API gateway, the admission check verifies the grant’s cryptographic integrity, scope, and expiration while querying the current lifecycle status and generation of every ancestor in the bound lineage path. If any ancestor has been canceled or its generation has advanced, the gateway rejects the descendant’s request immediately, even if the leaf worker has not yet received a cancellation signal and still considers itself active. Traversing a lineage path of depth \(\ell\) requires \(\mathcal{O}(\ell)\) ancestor lookups at admission time, whereas advancing an ancestor’s generation number invalidates the entire subtree of descendant grants in \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) time without rewriting individual token payloads. Gateway caches or indexed summaries can shorten those admission lookups at the cost of cache-invalidation rules, and table 8 traces how the check behaves across migration_task_01 without assuming that cancellation messages arrive everywhere at once.

Enforcing cancellation semantics requires an unambiguous, linearizable ordering point. Under a strict freshness policy, revocation updates and admission checks share an authoritative global order: once a revocation is committed, no subsequent admission check within that scope may evaluate against stale policy state. Because local signature verification and expiry checks establish only that a grant was valid when issued, a replicated gateway that cannot verify current ancestor state must fail closed, withholding admission from new operations.

Alternatively, systems that prioritize availability over strict consistency may permit distributed gateways to evaluate requests against cached ancestor state for a declared interval. This engineering trade-off introduces a stale-policy window, the bounded interval in which an already-revoked grant may still be accepted by an out-of-date gateway. The system designer must explicitly bound this window to account for cache invalidation propagation, token refresh intervals, clock skew assumptions, and network partition timeouts. Furthermore, revocation must actively sever all mediation pathways, including revoking open writable file handles held by running processes. Any operations already admitted to external systems prior to revocation remain governed by transactional settlement rules.

18.8.3 Approval requests and operation binding

During execution, a worker may discover that completing its objective requires an operation that exceeds its delegated authority. Rather than terminating or attempting privilege escalation, the worker submits an authorization request. An approval binds to the exact operation it authorizes, never to a model’s explanation of it (Action-bound approval), and the runtime recomputes that binding and rechecks its preconditions at dispatch (Dispatch-time rechecks). For a delegated publication, the bound operation names the originating task (migration_task_01), the content hash of the candidate artifact, the destination reference, and its base-version preconditions, so a candidate altered by a single byte after approval needs a fresh decision.

Delegation changes where the request goes and what the recheck must cover. The worker cannot widen its own grant, and attenuation forbids its parent from granting more than the parent holds. The runtime therefore collects such requests in a bounded approval queue, deduplicates repeated submissions from siblings, and routes each to the task owner rather than to an intermediate parent whose own grant could not cover the operation. Any rationale a root or worker model attaches remains unverified commentary. At dispatch, the recheck extends from the operation to its lineage: current grants, every ancestor’s lineage generation, and the remaining budget must still permit the action, so an ancestor cancellation committed after approval blocks it as surely as a changed candidate does. Passing validation tests in migration_accept_v1, obtaining operator approval, and executing the privileged publication remain three distinct, non-fungible events.

Checkpoint 18.3: Bounding work, authority, and spending after a split

Delegation controls conserve authority and spending, distinguish cancellation from completed cleanup, and separate administrative approval from verified success. These safeguards make task decomposition governable. Whether splitting a task across multiple agents is actually useful, however, remains a separate empirical question, answerable only by measurement against a strong single-agent baseline.

18.9 Benchmarking Against a Single Agent

When benchmarking the authentication migration (migration_task_01), granting parallel tool execution to the multi-agent supervisor while artificially restricting the single controller to serial execution measures a baseline handicap rather than the true benefit of delegation. A rigorous evaluation compares the supervisor against a single controller that already dispatches parallel searches and tests, beginning with an objective declared in advance, such as minimizing cost per accepted task subject to a deadline, or maximizing completed tasks within a fixed operating budget. The coordination tax (principle 14) then appears in three measurements read together: accepted-task rate, elapsed wall-clock time, and cost per accepted task. While a Pareto improvement across all three dimensions is ideal, it is rarely achieved. In practice, delegating work across agents represents an explicit trade of computational cost for lower latency. This trade-off is justified only when the engineering objective values latency reduction and the solution satisfies all quality and budget constraints.

18.9.1 Matched comparison conditions

Comparing a multi-agent fleet against a single-turn prompt or a controller with lower tool concurrency credits delegation for resources the single agent was never allowed to use. A fair evaluation applies the matched-budget comparison established in Matched-budget comparison, which fixes the task distribution, criteria, and resource envelope before policies are compared. It also uses an equally capable baseline, an iterative single controller with parallel tool execution, rather than an isolated, single-turn model call. For delegation, the matched envelope must also fix starting fixtures, evidence access, and identical concurrency limits on both model calls and tool executions. Because either policy may terminate early, unconsumed budget represents an operational outcome rather than an experimental discrepancy. If a delegated design also alters underlying model selections or prompt formulations, the trial evaluates a compound bundle of variables, so isolating the architectural impact of delegation requires a controlled ablation. Finally, acceptance must be scored on the integrated end-to-end task rather than on worker subtasks, because an interface update that passes its local unit tests still fails the migration if the accompanying caller patch introduces breaking signature incompatibilities.

18.9.2 Outcome, latency, and cost metrics

Every attempted task must remain in the evaluation denominator; tasks that encounter timeouts, unhandled errors, or indeterminate verdicts cannot be discarded from the benchmark. When the operational contract treats late delivery as an outage, on-time acceptance must be recorded as a distinct metric alongside gross completion. End-to-end latency spans the full execution envelope, measured continuously from task arrival through task decomposition, sub-agent dispatch, intermediate reconciliation, final integration, and automated assessment. Calculating median latency solely over accepted tasks introduces severe survival bias by filtering out prolonged failures. Systems evaluation therefore tracks latency across all arrivals. A task still running at the observation cutoff is right-censored: its latency is known only to exceed the cutoff, so it enters the latency distribution as a lower bound rather than being dropped. This is the same refusal to discard unresolved outcomes that keeps pending and unknown verdicts in the evaluation denominator (The four-verdict rule).

Cost accounting must encompass the complete system boundary: supervisor orchestration, retrieval queries, verification suites, merge conflict reconciliation, failed exploratory attempts, canceled speculative branches, and environment cleanup. Token expenditures must distinguish input and output tokens at their respective market prices, while concurrent tool invocations must be billed by aggregate worker execution time, which frequently exceeds the wall-clock span of the critical path. The effective cost per accepted task divides this full cohort spend by the accepted count under the same matched-budget accounting, so an architecture with no accepted solutions still reports its spend and the zero count. The token inflation documented in war story 18.1 illustrates why rigorous cost accounting is needed before committing to a multi-agent deployment.

Napkin Math 18.6: How much acceptance a token multiple must buy
Problem: A delegated multi-agent architecture consumes tokens at the ratios reported for the research system: 15× the token volume of a standard chat interaction, compared to 4× for an autonomous single agent. Assuming a constant price per token, how much must task acceptance improve for delegation to break even on cost per accepted task, and over which baseline success rates is break-even mathematically achievable?

Math: Let \(\DelegationTokenMultiple\) be the token multiple, delegated tokens per attempt over single-agent tokens per attempt. Following equation 1.2, with cost proportional to tokens, the delegated cost per accepted task divided by the single-agent cost per accepted task is \(\DelegationTokenMultiple\,\SingleAgentAcceptanceProbability/\MultiAgentAcceptanceProbability\). With \(\DelegationTokenMultiple=\) 15× \(/\) 4× \(=\) 3.75×, economic break-even requires that \(\MultiAgentAcceptanceProbability =\) 3.75× \(\cdot\, \SingleAgentAcceptanceProbability\). Because probability is bounded (\(\MultiAgentAcceptanceProbability \le 1\)), break-even is possible only when the single-agent baseline achieves \(\SingleAgentAcceptanceProbability \le\) 26.7 percent. Even if the reported evaluation score gain of 90.2 percent translated directly into an acceptance rate multiplier of 1.90×, the effective cost per accepted task would remain 2.0× that of the single-agent baseline.

Systems insight: When delegation multiplies token consumption by a factor of \(\DelegationTokenMultiple\), it reduces effective cost \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) if and only if it scales task acceptance by more than \(\DelegationTokenMultiple\). At an expansion ratio of 3.75×, this economic efficiency can materialize only on difficult tasks where the single agent succeeds less than 26.7 percent of the time. On workloads where a single agent already succeeds reliably, delegation cannot be justified by cost efficiency. It must be justified as a purchase of reduced wall-clock latency or broader search coverage, valued explicitly by the system’s operational requirements.

18.9.3 Scaling models for worker count

Coherency cost turns added workers into lost capacity past the peak.

A matched trial is expensive, so before running one a team must decide which worker counts deserve a trial at all. The fixed-work scaling model of equation 1 does not replace that benchmarking; its role is to guide the search by identifying promising worker counts. Varying the worker count alters resource contention, sub-task dispatch boundaries, and inter-agent reconciliation costs simultaneously. Consequently, any empirical overhead curve holds strictly within the evaluated range and serves as a hypothesis beyond it. To reason about scaling limits systematically, classical systems engineering separates everything that keeps a fleet from ideal division into work that serializes (undivided planning, and any overhead that queues at a single resource) and pairwise reconciliation. These two mechanisms require different architectural remedies.

Theorem 18.1: Universal scalability law

For a fixed amount of work divided among \(\WorkerCount\) equally capable workers, Gunther’s universal scalability law (USL) (Gunther 2008) gives the relative capacity, the speedup over one worker, as equation 5, with its peak at equation 6.

\[ \text{Speedup}(\WorkerCount)=\frac{\WorkerCount}{1+\ContentionCoefficient(\WorkerCount-1)+\CoherencyCoefficient \WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1)} \tag{5}\]

\[ \WorkerCount^*=\sqrt{\frac{1-\ContentionCoefficient}{\CoherencyCoefficient}} \tag{6}\]

  • Contention (\(\ContentionCoefficient\)): The share of work that serializes, including undivided planning, final assessment, and queueing at single resources such as the supervisor or the integration queue.
  • Coherency (\(\CoherencyCoefficient\)): The cost of pairwise reconciliation. Each of the \(\WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1)\) ordered worker pairs may exchange state, re-read a peer’s output, or resolve a conflicting change, the same count as the mesh round in section 18.3.
  • Amdahl’s law (\(\CoherencyCoefficient=0\)): Capacity saturates at \(1/\ContentionCoefficient\) and never declines.
  • Retrograde region (\(\WorkerCount>(1-\ContentionCoefficient)/\CoherencyCoefficient\)): A positive \(\CoherencyCoefficient\) makes added workers harmful, not merely useless. Capacity peaks at \(\WorkerCount^*\) and falls below one, slower than a single worker, past this count. With the screening notebook’s \(\ContentionCoefficient=\) 0.20 and \(\CoherencyCoefficient=\) 0.02, every count from 41 workers up finishes later than the serial reference.

Dividing both the numerator and denominator of equation 5 by \(\WorkerCount\) yields \(1/[\ContentionCoefficient+(1-\ContentionCoefficient)/\WorkerCount+\CoherencyCoefficient(\WorkerCount-1)]\). This expression matches equation 1 directly when setting the parallel fraction \(\ParallelFraction=1-\ContentionCoefficient\) and the overhead function \(\CoordinationOverhead(\WorkerCount)=\CoherencyCoefficient(\WorkerCount-1)\). In elapsed-time terms, \(\ContentionCoefficient\) captures fixed serial work whose duration does not change as workers are added, whereas the screen’s linear penalty \(\WorkerOverheadSlope\) plays the role of \(\CoherencyCoefficient\) by capturing any elapsed delay that grows in proportion to \(\WorkerCount-1\). Multiplying top and bottom by \(\WorkerCount\) turns that linear elapsed-time penalty into the quadratic term \(\CoherencyCoefficient\WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1)\) in the denominator of equation 5. Two very different physical mechanisms can produce that same algebraic term. True pairwise state reconciliation generates \(\WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1)\) interactions across the fleet, and when that quadratic aggregate work is shared evenly across the \(\WorkerCount\) concurrent workers, each worker experiences an elapsed delay proportional to \(\WorkerCount-1\). However, a central supervisor that sequentially processes one extra dispatch or receipt for each additional worker also adds an elapsed delay proportional to \(\WorkerCount-1\), without any peer-to-peer communication at all. A fitted value of \(\CoherencyCoefficient\) therefore flags an elapsed overhead that scales with worker count, and engineers must still inspect traces to see whether the culprit is pairwise worker reconciliation or per-worker queuing at the coordinator. Applying the notebook’s assumed parameters, equation 6 places the continuous theoretical optimum at 6.3 workers, which evaluates to the discrete optimum of 6 workers identified in the screening notebook.

↪ The stationary-root analysis that locates the peak of a fixed partition’s speedup curve is developed in Optimal worker count under Amdahl's Law with coordination overhead.

The architectural remedy depends on the measured source of overhead. Supervisor queue time, dependency invalidations, and reconciliation work help distinguish serial processing from cross-worker dependency work, and a matched trial that changes one mechanism can test the diagnosis. A verified serial bottleneck may benefit from pipelining supervisory prompts, sharding shared integration queues, or asynchronous event loops. When dependency reconciliation dominates, a partition that reduces cross-agent synchronization may instead pay. Interface refactoring coupled to caller implementations provides one such dependency hazard; a research query with independent search paths provides a contrasting partition.

Lighthouse 18.1: Research agent (orchestrator-worker fan-out)
Workload: The research agent addresses open-ended, breadth-first queries. A lead orchestrator decomposes the inquiry into independent search paths, dispatches read-only subagents to retrieve and analyze external sources, and synthesizes their condensed findings. The builders of one production research system report that this breadth-first regime is where delegation helps most, in contrast to coding tasks whose tight interdependencies leave less work to run in parallel (Hadfield et al. 2025).

Scenario coefficients: Suppose lead-agent planning and final synthesis serialize \(\ContentionCoefficient=\) 0.10 of total execution, while subagents operate almost entirely without cross-communication (\(\CoherencyCoefficient=\) 0.002). Evaluating the scalability model yields an optimal fan-out of 21 subagents achieving 5.5× relative capacity. In contrast, the code repair agent’s high coherency limits its configuration to 6 workers at 2.3× capacity. Under these assumptions, the research partition permits a 3.5× wider effective worker fan-out. (These stylized coefficients illustrate the scaling regime rather than measured telemetry.)

Local constraint: Low coherency overhead permits wider fan-out under the supplied model, but token expenditure and context trust can still constrain deployment. At fixed per-worker work, token consumption scales linearly with fan-out, so the economic break-even threshold analyzed in notebook 18.6 may cap deployment before the modeled capacity peak \(\WorkerCount^*\). Furthermore, because worker receipts incorporate uncurated text from external web APIs, the bounded receipt protocol of section 18.4 limits returned context and keeps worker claims separate from runtime evidence. Source-derived fields and summaries remain untrusted inputs whose proposed uses require the security checks established for the tool boundary.

Systems insight: Under the supplied coefficients, the research partition supports wider fan-out than the repair partition. Dependency traces can establish whether interface isolation addresses the repair workload’s overhead; matched outcome and cost measurements determine whether wider research fan-out earns its additional spend. Admission controls bound that spend independently of the modeled capacity gain.

In production systems, empirical engineering inverts this analytical workflow. Rather than postulating coefficients, engineers conduct targeted benchmarking trials across a small set of practical worker counts (for example, \(\WorkerCount \in \{1, 2, 4\}\)) to estimate \(\ContentionCoefficient\) and \(\CoherencyCoefficient\). The resulting model fit projects the empirical capacity peak \(\WorkerCount^*\), identifying the next candidate configuration to benchmark. A large fitted coherency parameter \(\CoherencyCoefficient\) warrants inspecting traces and testing targeted interventions. Redesigning the task decomposition is justified when that evidence confirms that dependency reconciliation dominates; the coefficient alone does not establish the remedy.

18.9.4 A complete delegation decision

Once the scalability fit has nominated a configuration, the matched trial decides whether it earns its tax. Consider the evaluation results for a representative cohort of software migration tasks patterned after migration_task_01, evaluated under identical automated test suites (migration_accept_v1). Both configurations operate under identical constraints: matching tool and model concurrency limits, identical sandbox permissions, and equivalent token and financial ceilings. The experiment isolates the delegation architecture itself (how work is decomposed, dispatched, and integrated) rather than what the runtime allows.

Napkin Math 18.7: Buying deadline coverage with delegation
Problem: A prespecified screening protocol requires at least 80 accepted tasks before a final observation cutoff of 600 s, including at least 75 accepted before a strict deadline of 300 s, evaluated across 100 initiated tasks. Each architecture operates under a total cohort budget ceiling of $20. Which configuration satisfies this engineering screen?

Assumptions: Every trial reaches a terminal verdict with settled charges. Tasks may continue executing past the deadline up to the final cutoff and budget ceiling, but late completions do not count toward on-time acceptance. Illustrative pricing is $3 per million input tokens, $10 per million output tokens, and $0.01 per worker-second of metered sandbox, evaluation, and cleanup services, with no human intervention charges.

Table 9 sets the outcome counts beside the resources that produce their cost.

Math: Total spend sums input and output tokens multiplied by their respective rates, plus aggregate worker service time multiplied by its compute rate. Dividing this sum by total accepted tasks yields the effective cost per accepted task. Both architectures meet the gross acceptance target and stay within the budget ceiling, but only the delegated system satisfies the on-time completion threshold. Delegation incurs an incremental spend of $6 to secure 8 additional on-time completions, a marginal cost of $0.75 per additional on-time task, while inflating the overall cost per accepted task by 1.5×.

Systems insight: The screen advances the delegated configuration because the operational contract explicitly prioritizes deadline compliance over raw cost efficiency. Delegation does not Pareto-dominate the single-agent baseline; it trades dollar spend for wall-clock timeliness. The rational decision rule hinges on marginal value: delegate when an incremental on-time completion is worth at least $0.75, but retain the single controller when the objective is minimizing cost per completed task. Because speculative failures and canceled branches account for 33.3 percent of the multi-agent spend, this discarded work is a substantial cost component to investigate when reducing the coordination tax. The median row is the survivors-only statistic that section 18.9.2 warns against. It drops every task that was not accepted, so it flatters both configurations, and the decision deliberately rests on the on-time count instead. A production release decision further requires evaluating task diversity, paired statistical tests, and formal uncertainty bounds as detailed in Statistical Evaluation Rigor.

Whichever configuration the matched trial favors, a single capable controller or a delegated topology, the deployment must preserve end-to-end acceptance verification, fine-grained sandbox authority, and accounting for canceled speculative work. The scalability fit chooses which worker counts deserve a trial, and the matched trial prices those candidates against the declared objective. Skipping either step, or relaxing the runtime controls that keep handoffs, merges, reviews, and cancellations sound, exposes the deployment to predictable multi-agent failures.

↪ Choosing between a single controller and a delegated topology by pricing both against the same acceptance contract and objective is developed in When a bounded loop beats a single call.

18.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Every pitfall below comes from treating a delegation boundary as a guarantee that the runtime never actually enforced. Splitting work across agents does not by itself speed up completion, prevent semantic conflicts, decorrelate reviewer errors, or settle in-flight operations when a parent stops. Each of those assumptions carries a measurable cost in latency, token spend, or broken repository state.

Fallacy: More workers necessarily improve the task outcome.

Adding parallel workers introduces synchronization overhead, resource contention, and coordination traffic that quickly erode parallel speedup. In the fixed-partition screen (notebook 18.1), deploying eight workers consumes 0.440 of the serial execution time, compared to 0.433 achieved at an optimal configuration of 6 workers. Beyond this point, coherency cost dominates: under the universal scalability law with those same coefficients, scaling out to 41 workers runs slower than executing the workload on a single agent. Furthermore, economic cost escalates even when latency stalls. A multi-agent delegation multiple of 3.75× in token consumption pays for itself only on difficult tasks that an isolated single agent completes less than 26.7 percent of the time. Rather than assuming that parallelism is beneficial, system designers must benchmark candidate topologies under identical token budgets and time ceilings against a declared task objective.

Pitfall: Treating a clean merge as evidence that concurrent changes remain compatible.

A successful textual merge only verifies that concurrent workers avoided line collisions in shared files, leaving cross-file semantic dependencies completely unvalidated. Across a repository of 1,000 files where each worker performs 10 edits, two concurrent workers touch completely disjoint files 90.4 percent of the time. As a result, nearly every pair of concurrent patches merges cleanly from the perspective of version control, even when a caller and an interface worker introduce mutually incompatible API contracts. Coordination systems must validate semantic dependencies and test candidate integration trees within an isolated environment before guarded publication (section 18.5).

Fallacy: Agreement among reviewers proves correctness.

Consensus among language-model reviewers reflects the correlation of their errors rather than an objective proof of correctness. When multiple reviewer agents share underlying model families, prompt templates, or reasoning biases, their mistakes correlate positively. For instance, five reviewers with an individual false-approval rate of 20 percent would reach an erroneous majority verdict only 5.79 percent of the time under strict statistical independence. Under the assumed beta mixture, in which reviewers share each candidate’s difficulty, that false-majority rate jumps to 16.68 percent. Adding more reviewers of the same kind cannot eliminate this shared bias, because error rates quickly level off near an irreducible floor of 15.0 percent (section 18.6). Production architectures must treat subjective reviewer voting as an advisory signal and isolate it strictly from deterministic, guarded verification checks.

Pitfall: Closing the parent task and releasing its reservations as soon as cancellation is requested.

A cancellation request immediately closes admission for new subtasks, but active execution, external tool calls, and downstream token charges drain asynchronously. Discarded work is not a rounding error. In the delegation of table 9, failed attempts and canceled speculative branches account for $6 of $18 in delegated spend, and that table is complete only because every one of those charges stayed owned until it settled. If the parent task releases its accounting context and budget allocations the moment cancellation is requested, the trailing operations continue running without an owner, and their charges land outside any budget as untracked spend and leaked resources. Robust orchestrators close admission at the entry gates, broadcast cancellation signals downstream, and maintain accounting context and state reservations until all active operations, diagnostic evidence, and billing charges have fully settled under the settlement rule.

Fallacy: If each worker usually finishes on time, the fan-out usually finishes on time.

Synchronizing multiple concurrent workers behind an all-results barrier compounds tail latency exponentially. Because every worker must complete before the barrier can lift, the overall probability of on-time completion multiplies each individual worker’s success rate. With an individual on-time probability of 90 percent per worker, a fan-out of 8 workers completes within the deadline only 43.05 percent of the time, and barrier success falls below even odds once the fleet reaches 7 workers (section 18.7). Where the system contract accepts interchangeable or quorum-based results, implementing a stopping rule restores 96.19 percent. Where every subtask is strictly required, the orchestrator must reduce fan-out width to keep tail latency bounded.

18.11 Summary

Delegation turns one trajectory into several. Every boundary it adds must carry explicitly what a single trajectory carried implicitly: the inputs a worker may rely on, the authority and allowance it may spend, the evidence it must return, and the obligations that outlive it. The runtime, not the workers, owns those boundaries. It separates claims from execution records, revalidates dependencies before anything publishes, treats agreement among similar reviewers as one correlated signal, and keeps every admitted operation owned until it settles.

Whether those boundaries are worth building is a measurement question. Delegation lowers cost per accepted task only when it raises acceptance by more than it multiplies spend; otherwise it must be bought as reduced wall-clock time or broader coverage that the declared objective explicitly values. The relations in table 10 carry these decisions, each under the modeling assumptions stated where it is developed.

Key Takeaways: Delegation must earn and preserve its boundaries
  • Coordination overhead sets the useful fleet size: Serial execution constraints cap parallel speedup at \(1/\ContentionCoefficient\), while pairwise reconciliation costs cause effective system throughput to fall beyond \(\WorkerCount^*=\sqrt{(1-\ContentionCoefficient)/\CoherencyCoefficient}\). Shrinking serial planning and integration overhead raises the scalability ceiling far more than merely shaving latency off individual handoffs. Empirical profiling across realistic workloads, rather than casual inspection of isolated agent trajectories, must calibrate these parameters.
  • Task dependencies govern completion makespan: Communication topologies, scheduling precedence, and fault-supervision hierarchies address distinct systems requirements. Once a schedule attains its critical-path bound, further latency reductions require shortening that path; before it does, additional slots or reduced contention may also shorten runtime. Furthermore, any agent endowed with recursive delegation authority requires hard runtime limits on fan-out, nesting depth, and aggregate token reservations.
  • Delegation handoffs require explicit runtime contracts: A handoff envelope must establish immutable task inputs, granted execution authority, resource budgets, and required evidence before worker execution begins. The returned receipt must strictly isolate ground-truth execution records from the worker’s unverified self-assessment, truncating narrative explanations rather than audit telemetry whenever context limits bind.
  • Disjoint writes do not guarantee semantic compatibility: Non-overlapping file edits establish only that parallel workers avoided concurrent modification conflicts; they do not prove that combined changes preserve system-wide behavioral invariants. Safe publication requires revalidating dependencies against the latest branch head, testing the integrated candidate in isolation, and advancing branch pointers through conditional atomic updates.
  • Correlated reviewer errors impose an irreducible accuracy floor: When automated reviewers evaluate an intrinsically challenging artifact, shared model blind spots cause their errors to correlate, preventing majority-voting failure rates from dropping to zero. Beyond this floor, spending additional tokens on reviewers of the same kind yields negligible benefit. Runtimes must instead incorporate independent checks, such as deterministic test suites or static analyzers, because peer consensus cannot manufacture authoritative correctness.
  • Resource obligations outlive parent caller lifecycles: Canceling a parent agent halts the dispatch of new downstream tasks, but it does not automatically terminate remote tool operations, network sockets, or asynchronous child processes already in flight. Each in-flight bound has its own mechanism. Credit windows bound the volume of queued work and support mean-residence sizing. Attenuated grants and atomic reservations bound authority and spend. The settlement record, not cancellation or credit release, keeps every admitted effect owned until its outcome and charges settle.
  • Multi-agent execution must outperform a capable single-agent baseline: A meaningful evaluation benchmarks multi-agent architectures against an equally optimized single-agent controller provisioned with identical parallel tool access, context allowances, and wall-clock budgets. System accounting must price every failed attempt, speculative invocation, and rollback, justifying multi-agent coordination only when the resulting increase in task success or latency reduction offsets its marginal token overhead.

The coordination tax (principle 14) manifests in the serialized handoffs, validation barriers, and residual dependencies that remain after decomposing a task. Invariant closure (principle 1) mandates that these architectural boundaries maintain strict external validation and effect gating, ensuring that peer consensus among models is never mistaken for authoritative execution rights. Finally, trajectory-level encapsulation (principle 10) extends from one trajectory to a tree of them. Every child is a separately owned trajectory whose derived grants only narrow, whose consumable allowance is reserved against its ancestors’ shared allowance, and whose unsettled side effects remain accounted for by the runtime after its parent stops.

18.12 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The question of when dividing computational work yields a net speedup is older than autonomous software agents. Gene Amdahl’s 1967 argument established that serial execution fractions strictly cap the speedup achievable by adding processors (Amdahl 1967). Two years later, Ronald Graham showed that while greedy list scheduling lands close to the theoretical dependency bound, adding parallel resources can paradoxically increase total completion time (Graham 1969). Neil Gunther later unified these dynamics in the universal scalability law, introducing a pairwise coherency penalty to explain how crosstalk and contention turn throughput saturation into outright performance collapse (Gunther 2008). Across successive computing generations, systems architects repeatedly found that coordination overhead and coherency penalties, rather than the raw supply of workers, dictate practical scale.

Amdahl, Gene M. 1967. “Validity of the Single Processor Approach to Achieving Large Scale Computing Capabilities.” Proceedings of the April 18-20, 1967, Spring Joint Computer Conference on - AFIPS ’67 (Spring), AFIPS ’67 (spring), 483–85. https://doi.org/10.1145/1465482.1465560.
Erman, Lee D., Frederick Hayes-Roth, Victor R. Lesser, and D. Raj Reddy. 1980. “The Hearsay-II Speech-Understanding System: Integrating Knowledge to Resolve Uncertainty.” ACM Computing Surveys 12 (2): 213–53. https://doi.org/10.1145/356810.356816.
Hewitt, Carl, Peter Bishop, and Richard Steiger. 1973. “A Universal Modular ACTOR Formalism for Artificial Intelligence.” Proceedings of the 3rd International Joint Conference on Artificial Intelligence (IJCAI), 235–45.

Distributed artificial intelligence confronted the handoff problem in the 1970s. The Hearsay-II speech-understanding system coordinated independent, opportunistic knowledge sources through a shared blackboard (Erman et al. 1980). Carl Hewitt’s actor model abandoned shared memory entirely, giving each autonomous agent private local state and routing interactions through addressed messages (Hewitt et al. 1973). Reid Smith formalized structured delegation in the Contract Net Protocol, turning task announcement, bidding, and award into an explicit, typed exchange between a manager and prospective contractors (Smith 1980).

Over the following decades, classical distributed systems research supplied the precise operational controls that multi-agent architectures require today. Kung and Robinson introduced optimistic concurrency control, permitting workers to execute tentatively against private workspace copies and validate isolation before committing (Kung and Robinson 1981). Birrell and Nelson established typed remote procedure calls with explicit failure semantics, replacing unstructured message passing with formal client-server contracts (Birrell and Nelson 1984). Gray and Cheriton solved distributed state synchronization and lock management through time-bounded leases (Gray and Cheriton 1989). Joe Armstrong formalized supervision trees within Erlang, demonstrating how structured hierarchies and crash-only semantics allow runtimes to restart failed processes from clean, known states (Armstrong 2003). Finally, object-capability systems eliminated ambient authority by embedding access rights directly into object references rather than relying on global identity checks (Dennis and Van Horn 1966; Hardy 1988), an approach later extended through cryptographically attenuable tokens with chained caveats (Birgisson et al. 2014).

Kung, H. T., and John T. Robinson. 1981. “On Optimistic Methods for Concurrency Control.” ACM Transactions on Database Systems 6 (2): 213–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/319566.319567.
Birrell, Andrew D., and Bruce Jay Nelson. 1984. “Implementing Remote Procedure Calls.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (1): 39–59. https://doi.org/10.1145/2080.357392.
Gray, Cary G., and David R. Cheriton. 1989. “Leases: An Efficient Fault-Tolerant Mechanism for Distributed File Cache Consistency.” Proceedings of the Twelfth ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles (SOSP), 202–10. https://doi.org/10.1145/74850.74870.
Dennis, Jack B., and Earl C. Van Horn. 1966. “Programming Semantics for Multiprogrammed Computations.” Communications of the ACM 9 (3): 143–55. https://doi.org/10.1145/365230.365252.
Hardy, Norm. 1988. “The Confused Deputy: (Or Why Capabilities Might Have Been Invented).” ACM SIGOPS Operating Systems Review 22 (4): 36–38. https://doi.org/10.1145/60609.60611.
Birgisson, Arnór, Joseph G. Politz, Úlfar Erlingsson, Ankur Taly, Michael Vrable, and Mark S. Miller. 2014. “Macaroons: Cookies with Contextual Caveats for Decentralized Authorization in the Cloud.” Proceedings of the Network and Distributed System Security Symposium (NDSS).
Condorcet, Marquis de. 1785. Essai Sur l’application de l’analyse à La Probabilité Des décisions Rendues à La Pluralité Des Voix. Imprimerie Royale.
Neumann, John von. 1956. “Probabilistic Logics and the Synthesis of Reliable Organisms from Unreliable Components.” In Automata Studies, edited by Claude E. Shannon and John McCarthy, vol. 34. Annals of Mathematics Studies. Princeton University Press. https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400882618-003.
Eckhardt, Dave E., and Larry D. Lee. 1985. “A Theoretical Basis for the Analysis of Multiversion Software Subject to Coincident Errors.” IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering SE-11 (12): 1511–17. https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.1985.231895.

Redundancy followed a parallel trajectory of high theoretical promises followed by sobering empirical corrections. The Condorcet jury theorem (Condorcet, Marquis de 1785) and John von Neumann’s multiplexed majority organs (Neumann 1956) demonstrated that aggregating independent votes can synthesize near-perfect reliability from noisy components. Leslie Lamport, Robert Shostak, and Marshall Pease proved the rigorous mathematical bounds of Byzantine fault tolerance. Agreement in the unsigned-message protocol model survives arbitrary, even malicious, faults only while fewer than one third of participants are faulty (Lamport et al. 1982). However, the independence premise behind probabilistic majority-voting guarantees collapsed when applied to software. John Knight and Nancy Leveson demonstrated experimentally that independently written programs fail coincidentally on the same edge cases far more often than chance predicts (Knight and Leveson 1986). Dave Eckhardt and Larry Lee provided the analytical explanation: complex problem domains harbor intrinsically difficult input regions that induce correlated failures across diverse implementations (Eckhardt and Lee 1985).

Modern language-model systems have revived every one of these designs at the same time. Self-consistency uses majority voting across sampled reasoning paths (Wang et al. 2023), multi-agent debate structures iterative argumentation across model instances (Du et al. 2023), and conversational frameworks coordinate role-playing agents over structured communication graphs (Wu et al. 2023). In production, orchestrator-worker patterns have delivered capability gains on breadth-first research tasks, and their builders report roughly 15× the token consumption of a chat interaction to get them (Hadfield et al. 2025). Systematic empirical analyses of multi-agent failures reveal that the field has re-encountered classical distributed failure modes: ill-defined task boundaries, uncoordinated handoffs, and verification bottlenecks that silently propagate errors rather than catching them (Cemri et al. 2025).

Wang, Xuezhi, Jason Wei, Dale Schuurmans, Quoc Le, Ed Chi, Sharan Narang, Aakanksha Chowdhery, and Denny Zhou. 2023. “Self-Consistency Improves Chain of Thought Reasoning in Language Models.” The Eleventh International Conference on Learning Representations.
Du, Yilun, Shuang Li, Antonio Torralba, Joshua B. Tenenbaum, and Igor Mordatch. 2023. “Improving Factuality and Reasoning in Language Models Through Multiagent Debate.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2305.14325.
Wu, Qingyun, Gagan Bansal, Jieyu Zhang, Yiran Wu, Beibin Li, Erkang Zhu, Biyi Gao, et al. 2023. AutoGen: Enabling Next-Gen LLM Applications via Multi-Agent Conversation.
Hadfield, Jeremy, Barry Zhang, Kenneth Lien, Florian Scholz, Jeremy Fox, and Daniel Ford. 2025. How We Built Our Multi-Agent Research System. Anthropic Engineering Blog.
  • The Contract Net Protocol (Smith 1980). The foundational text on structured delegation. Smith frames task distribution as a typed, negotiated protocol, showing that the essential elements of modern task envelopes, from capability announcements to formal awards, were established long before language models existed.
  • An Experimental Evaluation of the Assumption of Independence in Multiversion Programming (Knight and Leveson 1986). The landmark controlled experiment that disproved the assumption of independent software failures. This paper provides the standard methodological blueprint for evaluating whether an ensemble of redundant agent reviewers yields genuine reliability gains or merely inflates inference cost.
  • Making Reliable Distributed Systems in the Presence of Software Errors (Armstrong 2003). Armstrong’s doctoral dissertation detailing the architecture of Erlang. It articulates the operational rationale for supervision trees, fail-stop process isolation, and restart strategies, mapping directly to how orchestrators should manage the lifecycles and blast radiuses of child agent trajectories.
  • A General Theory of Computational Scalability Based on Rational Functions (Gunther 2008). Gunther derives the universal scalability law directly from queueing models. The derivation clarifies the physical distinction between contention and coherency, explaining why serial bottlenecks and cross-agent communication require different architectural fixes.
  • Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail? (Cemri et al. 2025). An extensive empirical taxonomy analyzing real-world multi-agent failure traces. It serves as a field catalog of the specification gaps, uncoordinated handoffs, and verification failures that production runtime environments must actively prevent.
Smith, Reid G. 1980. “The Contract Net Protocol: High-Level Communication and Distributed Problem Solving in a Distributed Processor.” IEEE Transactions on Computers C-29 (12): 1104–13.
Gunther, Neil J. 2008. “A General Theory of Computational Scalability Based on Rational Functions.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:0808.1431.
Cemri, Mert, Melissa Z. Pan, Shuyi Yang, Lakshya A. Agrawal, Bhavya Chopra, Rishabh Tiwari, Kurt Keutzer, et al. 2025. Why Do Multi-Agent LLM Systems Fail?

18.13 Exercises

Every figure given below is a hypothetical problem input, not a measurement of a real system. Each problem ends in a design decision, and the calculation exists to justify it.

  1. Choosing the lever in a worker-count screen (Basic). A fixed work partition has divisible fraction \(\ParallelFraction=0.9\) and normalized overhead \(\CoordinationOverhead(\WorkerCount)=\WorkerOverheadSlope(\WorkerCount-1)\) with \(\WorkerOverheadSlope=0.01\). The resource ceiling permits at most \(20\) workers, and each added worker adds roughly the same token spend.
    1. Using equation 1, compute the normalized elapsed time at \(\WorkerCount=4\), \(8\), and \(16\), the integer count that minimizes it, and the speedup at that count. Compare the count with \(\sqrt{\ParallelFraction/\WorkerOverheadSlope}\).
    2. Two proposals compete for the same engineering effort: halving the per-worker overhead to \(\WorkerOverheadSlope=0.005\), or halving the serial work so that \(\ParallelFraction=0.95\). Compute the best integer count and its speedup under each proposal.
    3. Decide which proposal to fund once worker spend is counted, and state which proposal alone can ever deliver a speedup above \(10\times\).
  2. Hot files in a shared workspace (Basic). A repository holds \(\RepositoryFileCount=5{,}000\) files, and each worker writes \(\WorkerWrittenFileCount=20\) of them, chosen uniformly and independently.
    1. Using equation 4, compute the probability of at least one shared write for \(\WorkerCount=2\) and \(\WorkerCount=4\) workers, both exactly and by the exponential approximation. Find the smallest fleet for which a shared write is more likely than not.
    2. Each worker also edits one shared dependency manifest with probability \(0.3\), independently of its other writes. For four workers, compute the probability that at least two edit the manifest, and the probability of any shared write at all.
    3. A colleague proposes a single shared working directory for four workers because “collisions are under 40 percent.” Decide, and name the two hazards of section 18.5 that remain even when no two workers write the same file.
  3. Sizing a credit window for staleness (Basic). A producer emits candidate items at \(\ArrivalRate=2\) items per second. A consumer model writes a \(400\)-token critique of each item at \(50\) output tokens per second, and each returned record occupies at most \(\DataVolume=8{,}192\) bytes. An item is useful only if the consumer begins serving it within \(60\) s of admission. For initial sizing, screen a saturated credit window by requiring its modeled mean residence to be no greater than this interval; assume negligible transport time and full utilization at the stated service rate.
    1. Compute the consumer’s service rate \(\ServiceRate\), the backlog of an unbounded queue after five minutes, and the wait of the newest item behind that backlog.
    2. Using the full-window mean residence \(\CreditWindowCapacity/\ServiceRate\), find the largest integer window \(\CreditWindowCapacity\) that passes this sizing screen and its payload bound \(\CreditWindowCapacity\DataVolume\). Does passing the screen establish that every item begins service before its freshness deadline?
    3. Find the minimum number of identical consumers needed to keep pace with the producer and the largest shared window that passes the same mean-residence screen at their aggregate service rate. State how admission timestamps and a freshness check before service should handle an item that has waited too long. Explain why cancellation and settlement traffic must not draw on these data credits.
  4. A fourth slot or a shorter vertex (Intermediate). A migration plan has a shared specification \(\DependencyGraphVertex_1\) (\(30\) s); four module refactors \(\DependencyGraphVertex_2\) through \(\DependencyGraphVertex_5\) (\(60\), \(60\), \(90\), and \(40\) s), each depending only on \(\DependencyGraphVertex_1\); and an integration check \(\DependencyGraphVertex_6\) (\(50\) s) depending on all four refactors. Each vertex occupies one interchangeable slot, with no other delays.
    1. Compute \(\AggregateSlotWork\) and \(\CriticalPathDuration\) from equation 2, and the lower bound of equation 3 for \(\WorkerCount=2\), \(3\), and \(4\) slots.
    2. By assigning the four refactors to slots, find the shortest achievable makespan for each \(\WorkerCount\). Explain why the bound is not attained at \(\WorkerCount=2\).
    3. The team currently runs \(\WorkerCount=3\) and can afford either a fourth slot or engineering that shortens \(\DependencyGraphVertex_4\) to \(60\) s. Decide, and state what shortening \(\DependencyGraphVertex_4\) buys once the fourth slot exists.
  5. Pricing deadline coverage (Intermediate). Under matched resource ceilings, a single controller spends an expected \(\$0.40\) per attempt with \(\AcceptanceProbability=0.60\), and \(40\) of every \(100\) attempts are accepted by the deadline. A delegated configuration spends \(\$0.90\) per attempt with \(\AcceptanceProbability=0.80\), and \(72\) of every \(100\) attempts are accepted by the deadline. Spend includes failed attempts and canceled branches, and cost is proportional to tokens.
    1. Compute \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) for both configurations from equation 1.2, and their ratio.
    2. Find the token multiple \(\DelegationTokenMultiple\), the delegated acceptance that would restore break-even on \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), and the largest single-controller acceptance for which break-even is possible at all.
    3. Per \(100\) attempts, compute the marginal cost of each additional on-time acceptance. One service values an on-time accepted migration at \(\$1\), another at \(\$5\). Decide which configuration each should run.
  6. Hedged lookups for the support agent (Intermediate, second-lighthouse transfer). The support agent answers a refund-eligibility question by dispatching three read-only lookups in parallel: the customer record, the order history, and the governing policy clause. All three are required. Each lookup attempt independently returns an admissible result within \(1\) s with probability \(0.70\) and within \(2\) s with probability \(0.95\). The service target is that at least \(95\) percent of answers have all three results within \(2\) s. Assume \(1{,}000\) sessions dispatch their three initial lookups simultaneously. The lookup pool has \(4{,}000\) slots, and each launched attempt retains its reservation until the \(2\) s observation cutoff, even if its result arrives earlier. An attempt that has not returned an admissible result remains outstanding until that cutoff. Treat the stated completion probabilities as unchanged by load when calculating a policy without admission blocking.
    1. Compute the probability that all three results arrive within \(2\) s without hedging. Explain why the \(\RequiredResultCount\)-of-\(\WorkerCount\) stopping rule of section 18.7 does not rescue this fan-out.
    2. Compare two hedging policies: duplicating every lookup at dispatch, and starting a replacement only for a lookup still outstanding at \(1\) s, which must then itself return within the remaining \(1\) s. Assuming every proposed hedge is admitted, compute the per-lookup and all-three probabilities within \(2\) s. For each policy, compute both expected slot demand and the largest possible slot demand before the cutoff.
    3. Decide whether either calculation establishes that the policy meets the deadline target within the hard slot limit. Propose an admission rule for delayed hedges, and explain why deadline acceptance must be evaluated again when the rule can deny replacements. Contrast the binding constraint here with the coherency limit on the repair agent’s migration, and state what must hold before the same hedging could be applied to issuing the refund itself.
  7. Bounding a remediation delegation tree (Intermediate, second-lighthouse transfer). The remediation agent is restoring a degraded checkout service. Its root grant permits reading metrics and logs for checkout and scaling the checkout deployment between \(4\) and \(12\) replicas; it permits no configuration writes. The root’s remaining allowance is \(\$50\), of which \(\$5\) is a protected finalization reserve and \(\$6\) is already reserved for the root’s own outstanding work. The model proposes three children, each with a \(\$20\) allowance, “less than the parent’s remaining \(\$50\)”: a metrics worker that reads checkout metrics, a log worker that reads logs for checkout and payments, and a capacity worker that scales checkout between \(4\) and \(16\) replicas.
