    1. State which proposed grants violate \(\GrantAllowedAccesses(\ChildCapabilityGrant)\subseteq\GrantAllowedAccesses(\ParentCapabilityGrant)\) and how to narrow them. Compute the worst-case liability if each child’s allowance is copied from the parent’s balance, and which children atomic reservation admits if they are dispatched in the order listed. Find the largest equal allowance that admits all three. The capacity worker then proposes two scale workers at \(\$8\) each; state which the runtime admits and repair the split.
    2. An operator takes over and the runtime cancels the root at \(\TimeInstant=0\). Both scale workers are active, and each submits at most one scale step every \(5\) s to a remote gateway. Under a cached-authorization policy the gateway trusts a lineage record for up to \(18\) s, with a \(2\) s clock-skew allowance. Under a strict policy it looks up the current status and generation of each of the \(3\) nodes in a scale worker’s lineage at \(4\) ms per lookup. Compute the stale-policy window, the most scale steps it can admit after cancellation, and the strict policy’s added latency as a fraction of the step interval. Choose a policy for scale steps and one for metric reads, and state what the strict policy costs if the authoritative store is unreachable for \(30\) s.
    3. Under the policy chosen in part (b), the cost record at cancellation shows: the metrics worker settled \(\$4\) and stopped; the log worker settled \(\$9\), and a log-export job it started before cancellation is still unresolved with a bounded liability of \(\$3\); the first scale worker settled \(\$2\) and was confirmed terminated; the second scale worker settled nothing, and a node-pool scale-up it started before cancellation has an unknown outcome with a bounded liability of \(\$5\); the root’s own work settled \(\$4\). Compute the allowance the runtime may release now, the amount still held, and the release a “cancellation refunds all reservations” rule would claim. A follow-up remediation task needs a \(\$20\) allowance. Decide whether to admit it now, and state who owns the two open operations and what that owner must retain.
  8. Buying evidence past a correlated floor (Advanced). Reviewers each falsely approve a known-invalid candidate with marginal probability \(\MarginalFalseApprovalProbability=0.10\), and their errors follow the beta mixture of section 18.6 with \(\CorrelationCoefficient=0.30\). The release rule requires that an invalid candidate be approved with probability at most \(0.01\). A reviewer call costs \(\$0.20\). A protected regression test costs \(\$0.50\) and passes an invalid candidate with probability \(0.05\), assumed independent of the candidate’s reviewer propensity \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\).
    1. Compute the beta parameters \(\PropensityPriorApprovalShape\) and \(\PropensityPriorRejectionShape\), and the majority false-approval probability for three reviewers under independent errors and under the mixture.
    2. Compute the mixture’s majority false approval for seven reviewers and the floor \(\Pr(\FalseApprovalPropensity>1/2)\), numerically. Decide whether any number of reviewers of this kind can meet the release rule.
    3. Price two designs per candidate: seven reviewers under majority vote, and three reviewers plus the protected test, with approval requiring both a reviewer majority and a passing test. Compute each design’s false-approval probability and choose one.
    4. Critique the assumption that the test is independent of \(\FalseApprovalPropensity\). Name a mechanism from this chapter that would correlate the test with the reviewers, and state the measurement that must precede release.
  9. Fitting the scalability law before choosing a fan-out (Advanced, critique and redesign). Trials of a repair-migration partition under matched resources measure relative capacity \(\text{Speedup}(2)=1.835\) and \(\text{Speedup}(4)=2.878\). The serial reference takes \(40\) minutes, and the service requires completion within \(11\) minutes. The expected cost of an attempt grows with worker count as \(\$1.00+\$0.30(\WorkerCount-1)\), and acceptance does not change with \(\WorkerCount\).
    1. Rearrange equation 5 so that it is linear in \(\ContentionCoefficient\) and \(\CoherencyCoefficient\), fit both coefficients from the two measurements, and compute \(\WorkerCount^*\) from equation 6, the best integer count and its capacity, and the smallest count that runs slower than one worker.
    2. A trial at \(\WorkerCount=8\) measures \(2.90\). Compare it with the fitted prediction, and state what the gap implies about using the fit outside the measured range.
    3. The team proposes deploying the best integer count from part (a). Critique the proposal against the deadline. For this calculation, suppose two proposed redesigns have the following responses: sharding the integration queue halves \(\ContentionCoefficient\), and repartitioning the migration so interface changes land before caller work begins halves \(\CoherencyCoefficient\). For each, find the smallest worker count that meets the deadline, if any, and its cost per attempt, and recommend a design under these assumptions. State what trace or matched intervention evidence would establish the assumed coefficient responses in a deployment.

What’s Next: From coordination to cost
When is the time saved by delegation worth its additional resource use? This chapter answered for one stated workload, charging failed attempts and canceled branches to the accepted tasks they served. That accounting is a special case of a question every deployment faces, for one agent or many. The next chapter opens with the repair team offered a model whose tokens cost a tenth as much and asks why the switch can still raise the price of every repair that ships. Answering it means counting what each accepted task costs across all its attempts, how context reuse, retries, and model choice change that cost, and how concurrent trajectories compete for capacity. The same account then prices delegation itself, without dropping the reservations and unresolved obligations established here.
